Green: "the eye and the mind repose on the result of this junction as upon a simple colour"; hence rooms "to live in constantly". (read, §§778-802)
- §§915-919, application of colour: "symbolical" (colour used according to its own effect, for example pure red for majesty), "allegorical" (a meaning imposed by convention, "what idea ... is attached to the green colour, which has been appropriated to hope?"), and "mystical": "The mathematician extols the value and applicability of the triangle; the triangle is revered by the mystic ... we presently arrive at the ancient mysterious hexagon." Yellow and blue's union into red, he says, calls to mind "the earthly" (green) and "heavenly" (red) "generation of the Elohim". (read)
- I recall that Goethe also drew a rose or circle matching colours to the four temperaments (1798-99), and that planets do not appear in it; I did not open it. (memory)

##### Wassily Kandinsky, Concerning the Spiritual in Art (1911)

Astrology: no. I searched the text for "planet", "Saturn", "Mars" and "astrolog" and found none.

Edition opened: M. T. H. Sadleir's translation (1914), Project Gutenberg text 5321 as mirrored at https://archive.org/download/concerningthespi05321gut/cnspr10.txt. Chapter VI is "The Language of Form and Colour".

- Form and colour together: "A yellow triangle, a blue circle, a green square, or a green triangle, a yellow circle, a blue square--all these are different and have different spiritual values." "On the whole, keen colours are well suited by sharp forms (e.g., a yellow triangle), and soft, deep colours by round forms (e.g., a blue circle)." And: "A triangle ... has a spiritual value of its own ... The case is similar with a circle, a square, or any conceivable geometrical figure." A footnote adds that the angle and whether the figure is moving or still matter. (read)
- Warm and cold: "Yellow ... Blue" as the first antithesis with a movement toward and away from the spectator ("yellow ... a spreading movement out from the centre, and a noticeable approach to the spectator. The blue ... moves in upon itself, like a snail retreating into its shell"); white and black as the second antithesis; green, red and then orange and violet as further pairs. (read)
- Single colours: yellow "the typically earthly colour. It can never have profound meaning"; blue "the typical heavenly colour", ending in "rest"; green "the most restful colour that exists ... the 'bourgeoisie'"; white "a great silence ... pregnant with possibilities"; black "something burnt out, like the ashes of a funeral pyre"; red "a determined and powerful intensity"; orange "like a man, convinced of his own powers"; violet "a cooled red ... rather sad and ailing". Each is given a musical instrument (for example "a light blue is like a flute", red "a sound of trumpets"). (read)

##### Wassily Kandinsky, Point and Line to Plane (1926)

Astrology: no.

Edition opened: the 1947 English edition from the Museum of Non-Objective Painting (translators I recall as Howard Dearstone and Hilla Rebay, from memory; the scan contains an article by Rebay), scanned at https://archive.org/download/pointlinetoplane00kand/pointlinetoplane00kand_djvu.txt (the scan carries double spacing; the passages below are on the pages numbered 71-72 in that edition, in the section "Angular Lines and Colour").

- "The cold-warm of the square and its definite plane-like nature, immediately become sign-posts pointing to red ... It is not, therefore, completely without justification that the right angle is placed on a parallel with red." (read)
- "an angle of 60° ... When the openings of two such angles are brought together, they produce an equilateral triangle — three sharp, active angles — and become the sign-post to yellow. Thus, the acute angle has a yellow colour within." (read)
- "The obtuse angle increasingly loses its aggression ... gives this angle a light blue tone ... the obtuse (150°) angle ... is a typical blue angle and is a presentiment of the curved line which, in its further course, has the circle as its final goal." (read)
- Lines: "diagonal, red (or grey, or green)"; "free straight line, yellow and blue"; and the circle "tends most toward colourless rest". Kandinsky refers back to "On the Spiritual in Art, p. 67, Figure II" and to the Bauhaus book of 1923. (read)

##### Johannes Itten, The Art of Color (German Kunst der Farbe, 1961; English edition 1973)

Astrology: partly; two passing mentions of a symbol and a planet, no system.

Edition opened: the English edition (the scan states copyright 1961 and 1973 by Otto Maier Verlag; the English publisher and translator I recall as Van Nostrand Reinhold and Ernst van Haagen, from memory), scanned at https://archive.org/download/johannes-ittens-the-art-of-color/Johannes%20Ittens%20%E2%80%93%20THE%20ART%20OF%20COLOR_djvu.txt (pp. 119-120 for "Form and Color"; the Mars remark in the section on red-orange).

- "As is true of the three primary colors, red, yellow and blue, the three fundamental shapes - square, triangle and circle - may be assigned distinct expressive values." The square "symbolizes matter, gravity and sharp limitation ... The square corresponds to red, the color of matter." The triangle, whose "acute angles produce an effect of pugnacity and aggression", "is the symbol of thought, and among colors its weightless character is matched by lucid yellow." The circle: "the symbol of the spirit, moving undivided within itself ... The incessantly moving circle corresponds among colors to transparent blue." (read)
- "The astrological symbol for the sun is a circle with a dot in the center." (read; in the circle paragraph)
- On red-orange: "Associated with the planet Mars, red is bound to the burning worlds of war and demons. It was worn as a sign of martial occupation by warriors in combat." (read)
- The same book builds harmony from the twelve-hue colour circle by figures: "all complementary pairs, all triads whose colors form equilateral or isosceles triangles in the twelve-member color circle, and all tetrads forming squares or rectangles, are harmonious". The yellow, red, blue triad is "an equilateral triangle". (read)

##### Paul Klee, Pedagogical Sketchbook (1925; English 1953)

Astrology: no.

Edition opened: the 1953 English text (Sibyl Moholy-Nagy), scanned at https://archive.org/download/pedagogical-sketchbook-klee-1953/Pedagogical%20Sketchbook%20-%20Klee%20%281953%29_djvu.txt (short; about 39 kilobytes).

- The circle is "the purest of mobile forms" and "the cosmic one" when gravity is removed; it ends in "the spectral color circle where all the arrows are superfluous"; the sketchbook's own list of line themes ends with "Line as symbol of color mutations and kinetic harmony". I found no planet, sign or element in it. (read)

##### Rudolf Arnheim, Art and Visual Perception (1954; new version 1974)

Astrology: no.

Edition opened: scanned text at https://archive.org/download/art-and-visual-perception-rudolf-arnheim/%21%20Art%20and%20Visual%20Perception%20-%20Rudolf%20Arnheim%20_djvu.txt (the colour chapter).

- Arnheim reports Kandinsky's teaching and doubts it: "When Kandinsky taught in his Bauhaus seminar that the color yellow was akin to the shape of a triangle, blue to a circle, and red to a square, was he voicing more than a personal impression?" He adds that such attributions "are so heavily overlaid with personal or cultural factors that they cannot claim much general validity." (read)
- He also notes: "A particularly attractive design of the overall system was done by Paul Klee for his students at the Bauhaus. He called it 'Canon of Color Totality.'" (read)
- A table in the notes records a classroom test of whether observers matched shapes and colours; the scan was too broken to quote reliably. (not read)

##### Rudolf Steiner, Hilma af Klint, Scriabin, the temperaments

- Steiner. I did not open his colour lectures (Colour, CW 291). A search summary says the 1921 lectures link mineral colours to the Moon's relation to the Earth and mention Saturn, Sun and Moon only as earlier evolutionary stages; I recall that he treats colour as lustre, image and living colour and does not set up a planet-colour table. (memory)
- Hilma af Klint. Search-result summaries (museum pages for The Ten Largest, 1907) say she used blue for the female, yellow for the male and pink or red for love, and shifted from blue grounds in the childhood paintings to orange, purple and ochre later. I did not open her notebooks or any text of her own. (memory)
- Scriabin. The Wikipedia article on the colour keyboard ("Clavier à lumières") lists his key colours (C intense red, D yellow, E sky blue, G orange, A green, and so on) and says that in the circle-of-fifths order they run through the spectrum, influenced by "theosophic readings" and Newton's Optics as quoted by Castel; it mentions no planets or astrology. (read: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clavier_%C3%A0_lumi%C3%A8res)
- The four temperaments. The Wikipedia article ties sanguine to air, choleric to fire, melancholic to earth, phlegmatic to water; it names no planets and no colours for them. (read: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_temperaments)

#### Section 6. Where the authors differ, side by side

##### Table 1: the colour of each planet

Cells are the source's own words, shortened. "–" means I found nothing in the text I opened.

| Planet | Plato (Rep. X) | Valens I.1 | Biruni §401 | Hephaistio I.24 (colour seen in eclipse) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Saturn | yellower, like Mercury | like castor | jet-black; black mixed with yellow; lead; pitch-dark | dark or sallow |
| Jupiter | whiteness second | gray verging on white | dust-colour, white mixed with yellow and brown, shining | white |
| Mars | reddish | red | dark red | reddish |
| Sun | brightest | yellowish | shining reddish-yellow; colour of the lord of the hour | – |
| Venus | whitest | white | pure white tending to straw; some say greenish | yellow |
| Mercury | yellower, like Saturn | blue (supplied by the translator) | sky-blue mixed with a darker colour | many-coloured |
| Moon | colour of reflected sunlight | green | blue and white, or a deep colour mixed with reddish yellow | – |

##### Table 2: the colour of each planet, Renaissance to twentieth century

| Planet | Agrippa I.49 | Ficino III.11, III.19 | Lilly, Book I (table; sky colour) | Alan Leo (1913) | 777 King scale | 777 Queen scale |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Saturn | black, lucid, earthy, leaden | – | black; pale, wan, leaden, ashy | green | indigo | black |
| Jupiter | sapphire, airy, ever-green, clear, purple, dark, gold mixed with silver | sapphire-blue (and green, gold-silver) | red and green mixed; bright, clear, azure | blue | violet | blue |
| Mars | red, burning, fiery, violet, purple, bloody, iron | – | red or iron; shining, fiery, sparkling | red | scarlet | red |
| Sun | golden, saffron, purple, bright | blazing yellow, pure gold, lighter purple | yellow or yellow-purple (scan: "yellow or yellow. Purple") | orange | orange | yellow (gold) |
| Venus | with Mercury and Moon: white, fair, green, ruddy, between saffron and purple | white, green, a bit yellowish; "green is the color of Venus and also of the Moon" | white or purple; bright shining | indigo | emerald or green | sky blue |
| Mercury | as Venus | as Venus | sky colour or bluish | yellow | yellow | purple |
| Moon | as Venus | as Venus; green | spotted white and mixed | violet | blue | silver |

Notes. Alan Leo's order reads "indigo, violet, and blue of Venus, Moon, and Jupiter ... yellow, orange, and red of Mercury, Sun, and Mars ... the green of Saturn". Itten alone ties one planet to one colour, Mars to red. Ficino III.19 also names gold for the Sun, "not alien to Jupiter and Venus".

##### Table 3: the colour of each sign

| Sign | Lilly (1647) | 777 King scale | 777 Queen scale (OCR table) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Aries | white mixed with red | scarlet | red |
| Taurus | white mixed with citrine | red-orange | deep indigo |
| Gemini | white mixed with red | orange | pale mauve |
| Cancer | green or russet | amber | maroon |
| Leo | red or green | yellow with a tinge of green | deep purple |
| Virgo | black speckled with blue | yellowish green | slate grey (prose: slate-green) |
| Libra | black or dark crimson, or tawny | emerald | blue |
| Scorpio | brown | greenish (Prussian) blue | dull brown |
| Sagittarius | yellow or green sanguine | blue | yellow |
| Capricorn | black, russet or swart brown | indigo | black |
| Aquarius | sky colour with blue | violet | sky blue |
| Pisces | white glistering | crimson | buff flecked silver-white |

Agrippa gives colours to the twelve houses, in pairs, rather than to the signs (white 1 and 7; green 2 and 12; saffron 3 and 11; red 4 and 10; honey 5 and 9; black 6 and 8). Biruni's sign-colour table could not be read in the scan.

##### Table 4: the element of each planet

| Planet | Agrippa I.8 | Agrippa I.24-29 | Ptolemy I.4 (quality, not element) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Saturn | watery | earth and water | cold, somewhat dry |
| Jupiter | airy | air | temperate, hot and moist |
| Mars | fiery | fire | dry and burning |
| Sun | fiery | – | hot, somewhat dry |
| Venus | airy | air and water | tempered, chiefly moist |
| Mercury | watery | water | drying and moist by turns |
| Moon | earthy ("by many ... watery") | earth, then water | moist, somewhat warm |

##### Table 5: bodily form given to the planets

| Planet | Ptolemy III.11 | Biruni §428 | Lilly (Book I) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Saturn | dark, robust, black curly hair, middling stature (rising); dark, slender, small, black-eyed (setting) | ugly, tall, wizened, large head, small eyes, short neck, crooked legs, big feet | spare, lean, short or middling, pale or swarthy, little black eyes, broad forehead, thick lips and nose |
| Jupiter | fair with good colour, large eyes, tall | fine figure, round face, large eyes, curly reddish hair | – (not read) |
| Mars | red and white, tall, robust, gray-eyed | tall, large head, small eyes, long fingers | full-faced, high colour, sparkling yellow eyes, reddish hair, scar, broad shoulders, stout |
| Sun | – (assists the others) | large head, white inclining to yellow, large paunch | obscure white mixed with red, round face, short chin, curling hair |
| Venus | shapely, plump, graceful, bright eyes | round face, reddish-white, medium tall, short fingers | round face, full eye, red lips, well shaped, inclined to shortness |
| Mercury | sallow, graceful, small eyes (rising) | brown with greenish tinge, narrow forehead | brown or dark yellowish, long face, thin sharp nose, slender, small legs |
| Moon | – | clear white, erect, round face | – (not read) |

##### Table 6: colour, shape and feeling in design

| Author | Triangle | Circle | Square | Other statements |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kandinsky 1911 | yellow, "sharp" | blue, "soft, deep" | green is paired with it in the first example | yellow earthly; blue heavenly; any colour with any figure is possible and gives a different value |
| Kandinsky 1926 | sharp angle: yellow | obtuse angle and curve: blue | right angle: red | diagonal red; horizontal and vertical a "coloured rest" |
| Itten (1961) | yellow, thought, aggression | blue, spirit, motion | red, matter, gravity | harmonies drawn as triangles, squares and rectangles on the colour circle |
| Goethe 1810 | revered by the mystic; the colour hexagon | – | – | plus side yellow to red: lively; minus side blue to red-blue: anxious |
| Klee 1925 | – | purest mobile form | – | colour circle; line as symbol of colour changes |
| Arnheim 1954/74 | doubts the Kandinsky pairing as personal | | | colour attributions "cannot claim much general validity" |
| Agrippa 1533 (astrology) | – | binding, conjuration, unity and ten | the cross: four right angles; earth a cube | pentangle against evil spirits |

#### What I could not find or open

- Dorotheus of Sidon, Carmen Astrologicum: the English translation is in print (Pingree) and Dykes's version exists; I found only partial free copies and read none. I do not know whether either gives planet colours.
- Firmicus Maternus, Mathesis: I did not locate a free English text, and I did not read it. I recall that its planet and sign chapters describe bodies and temperaments, but I cannot quote it. (memory)
- Abu Ma'shar (Abu Ma'shar al-Balkhi), Great Introduction and its abridgement: I did not open a text. The 1488 Latin incunable (Flowers of Abu Mashar) was available in scan, but I did not read it. Biruni, which I did read, draws on this tradition; I cannot say which of his lists are Abu Ma'shar's.
- Abraham ibn Ezra, Beginning of Wisdom (Reshit Ḥokhmah): only catalogue entries and the news of Shlomo Sela's 2017 edition; no text of the planet chapter.
- Al-Biruni's sign colour table (§362) and his metals list: the scan is too damaged to read as a table.
- Alan Leo, Esoteric Astrology: the table of signs, colours and tones (p. 67): signs unreadable in the scan.
- Alan Leo's other works (Astrology for All; Art of Synthesis): I downloaded Astrology for All but searched only for colour and glyph words and found none; I read nothing further of it.
- Paracelsus: only the short second treatise of Archidoxis Magica (the zodiac seals) in an extract; I did not read his planet seals or other works, and the seal drawings were lost.
- Lilly: Jupiter's, Moon's and some planetary bodies I did not read in the planet chapters; the glyphs in the colour table were garbled in the scan, though the order and counts are intact.
- Agrippa's actual magic-square seal drawings: the transcription gives the squares' sums and totals but the drawings are not in words; I could not see the shapes they make.
- Rudolf Steiner's colour lectures, Hilma af Klint's own writings, Goethe's temperament rose, Rudhyar, Mathers' Golden Dawn papers themselves: not opened (see the marks above).
- Itten, The Elements of Color (the second book) and Klee's Thinking Eye: lending-only on archive.org; I could not open them.
- Kandinsky's Bauhaus colour-shape questionnaire (1923): I know of it only through Arnheim's mention (read), and through Kandinsky's own footnote (read); I did not open the Bauhaus book.
- Valens in Riley's own PDF: I read only the re-typeset copy on GitHub.
- Pliny (Natural History II), Cicero (Somnium Scipionis) and Macrobius on planet colours: not opened.
- Palmistry beyond Cheiro (Benham, Hindu cheiromancy): not opened.

### Indian sources on shape, line, colour, design and architecture linked to the nine grahas, twelve rāśis, five mahābhūtas and three guṇas

A gathering of passages, not an argument. Each statement is marked as follows.

- **(read: URL)** means I opened the text at that address and read the passage myself, in the language shown.
- **(read, summary: URL)** means I opened the page through a fetch that returned a summary or a partial quotation instead of the raw text; I report only what it returned.
- **(search only: URL)** means I saw the claim in a search result and did not open the page.
- **(memory)** means I recall it and did not open it.

Terms used throughout: *graha* (planet, including the two lunar nodes), *rāśi* (zodiac sign), *mahābhūta* (great element), *guṇa* (strand of nature: *sattva* clarity, *rajas* activity, *tamas* inertia), *maṇḍala* (circle or enclosure; a diagram laid out for worship), *yantra* (instrument; a diagram), *vāstu* (site or dwelling), *varṇa* (colour; also the four social classes), *rasa* (aesthetic flavour, sentiment), *pīṭha* (seat, pedestal), *vāhana* (mount), *adhidevatā* (presiding deity), *dikpāla* (guardian of a direction), *śānti* (pacification), *citra* (painting), *tāla* (proportion measure), *aṅgula* (finger-breadth).

The core astrological texts (Parāśara, Varāhamihira, Kalyāṇavarman, Mantreśvara, Vaidyanātha) are not repeated here. Where a later author visibly follows one, I say so.

---

#### 1. The nine grahas on altars, pedestals and in images

##### 1.1 Yājñavalkyasmṛti (YājS), 1.293–308

Edition opened: the GRETIL electronic text, which numbers the verses as quoted. (read: http://gretil.sub.uni-goettingen.de/gretil/corpustei/transformations/html/sa_yAjJavalkyasmRti.htm)

Date: not given on the page; I did not open an introduction. (memory) The work is usually placed in the early centuries CE.

- 1.295: whoever wants prosperity, pacification, rain, long life or well-being should perform a *grahayajña* (sacrifice to the planets). (read: URL above)
- 1.296 names the nine: Sūrya, Soma, Mahīputra (Mars, "son of the Earth"), Somaputra (Mercury, "son of the Moon"), Bṛhaspati, Śukra, Śanaiścara, Rāhu, Ketu. (read: URL above)
- 1.297: *tāmrakāt sphaṭikād raktacandanāt svarṇakād ubhau / rājatād ayasaḥ sīsāt kāṃsyāt kāryā grahāḥ kramāt.* The images are made in order of copper, crystal, red sandalwood, gold (two of them, "ubhau"), silver, iron, lead, bell-metal. (read: URL above)
- 1.298: *svavarṇair vā paṭe lekhyā gandhair maṇḍalakeṣu vā / yathāvarṇaṃ pradeyāni vāsāṃsi kusumāni ca.* Or they are to be painted on cloth in their own colours, or (drawn) with scented pastes in maṇḍalas; cloths and flowers are to be given "according to colour". (read: URL above)
- The verses name no shape and no direction for any graha, and give no colour list in words; "their own colours" is left to the reader. Marianna Kropf (2005, p. 195, n. 45) says the same: the YājS leaves all detail of form, size, placing and direction unspecified, and the Mitākṣarā commentary on it cites the Matsyapurāṇa for the iconography and the directions. (read: https://list.indology.info/pipermail/indology/attachments/20260320/d9c10eae/attachment.pdf)

##### 1.2 Matsyapurāṇa (MaP), chapters 93–94

Edition opened: *Matsya Mahāpurāṇa*, vol. 1 (chapters 1–150), Parimal Publications, Delhi, Sanskrit text with English translation by a board of scholars, ed. K. L. Joshi. The scan's Sanskrit is badly recognised; the English is clean. (read: https://archive.org/details/pdfcoffee.com_matsya-purana--4-pdf-free) I took the Sanskrit of the key verses from the Sanskrit Wikisource page for chapter 93, which a fetch returned in part. (read, summary: https://sa.wikisource.org/wiki/मत्स्यपुराणम्/अध्यायः_९३)

Date: not established by anything I read.

Chapter 93, *Navagraha yajña* (sacrifice to the nine planets):

- The altar (*vedī*) is made one *vitasti* (span) high and square, with its face to the north, in the north-east of the fire pit; a larger version for the "ten million" offering is a square four spans on a side with terraces (the Sanskrit gives *caturasrā samantāc ca tribhir vaprais tu saṃyutā*). (read: archive.org Parimal link above; verses 93.8 and 93.126–127 per the Wikisource page, read, summary)
- Placing, 93.11–12 (Sanskrit as the fetch returned it): *madhye tu bhāskaraṃ vindyāl lohitaṃ dakṣiṇena tu / uttareṇa guruṃ vindyād budhaṃ pūrvottareṇa tu // pūrveṇa bhārgavaṃ vindyāt somaṃ dakṣiṇapūrvake / paścimena śaniṃ vindyād rāhuṃ paścimadakṣiṇe / paścimottarataḥ ketuṃ sthāpayet śuklataṇḍulaiḥ.* The Sun in the middle; Mars south; Jupiter north; Mercury north-east; Venus east; Moon south-east; Saturn west; Rāhu south-west; Ketu north-west, "all drawn with uncooked rice powder" in the Parimal English. (read: Parimal link; read, summary: Wikisource link)
- Colours, 93.17 (as the fetch returned the Sanskrit; spelling not checked against an edition): the Sun with Mars red; Moon and Venus white; Mercury and Jupiter yellow-tawny (*piṅgala*); Saturn and Rāhu black; Ketu smoke-coloured. The Parimal English adds that the planets' "forms are of the same colour", that they are dressed in cloth and offered flowers of that colour. (read: Parimal link; read, summary: Wikisource link)
- Presiding deities listed in the same chapter (Parimal English): Śiva for the Sun, Pārvatī for the Moon, Skanda for Mars, Hari for Mercury, Brahmā for Jupiter, Indra for Venus, Yama for Saturn, Kāla for Rāhu, Citragupta for Ketu, then a second set of co-presiding deities (Agni, Water, Earth, Viṣṇu, Indra, Indra's wife, Prajāpati, Serpents, Brahmā), thirty-one in all, thirty-two with the two Aśvins. (read: Parimal link)

Chapter 94, *Description of the form of nine planets* (Parimal English):

- Sun: on a lotus, "colour like that of a lotus", seven horses. Moon: white, white robes, chariot and horse. Mars: red robes and garland. Mercury: yellow, yellow robes, a lion. Venus white and Jupiter yellow. Saturn: "the colour and the lustre of an emerald", a vulture. Rāhu: terrible face, a blue lion. Ketu: smoky, a vulture.
- Every image crowned; a measure of two fingers or 108 aṅgulas (the translator's note says the sense is obscure). (all read: Parimal link)

##### 1.3 Garuḍapurāṇa, Ācārakāṇḍa chapter 101 (Dutt translation)

Edition opened: M. N. Dutt's English translation as hosted on Wisdomlib, chapter 101 "Propitiation of malignant Planets". The page's text says: "The Sun is red; the Moon white; Mars red; Mercury and Jupiter yellowish; Venus white; all the rest being black." It gives the metals as copper, bell-metal, crystal, red sandalwood, gold, silver, iron, lead and bell-metal. (read: https://www.wisdomlib.org/hinduism/book/the-garuda-purana-dutt/d/doc122581.html) It says nothing of shapes or directions.

##### 1.4 Agnipurāṇa, chapters 51 and 164 (Gangadharan translation)

Edition opened: N. Gangadharan's translation as hosted on Wisdomlib. (read: https://www.wisdomlib.org/hinduism/book/the-agni-purana/d/doc1083393.html for chapter 164; https://www.wisdomlib.org/hinduism/book/the-agni-purana/d/doc1083139.html for chapter 51)

- Chapter 164, *navagraha-homa*, verses 2–7: images of copper, crystal, red sandalwood, gold, silver, iron and lead "respectively"; or of gold only; "or their figures may be drawn in the circles drawn with fragrant materials"; dress and flowers "matching their respective colours". No shape and no direction is given for the nine.
- Chapter 51, "Characteristics of the images of the Sun and other planets", verses 4–10: the twelve forms of the Sun, from Aryaman on, "placed in a diagram of twelve petals" (*dvādaśadala*, a twelve-petalled lotus diagram), each named for a month and for the zodiac sign the Sun is in; their female energies (*śakti*) at the petal ends have colours given in order as black, red, pale red, yellow, pale yellow, white, brown, yellow, green, white, grey and blue. The page's footnote ties the twelve names to the signs from Aries and the months from Mārgaśīrṣa. This is a twelve-fold colour sequence tied to the twelve rāśis in a diagram. The remaining verses give each of the other grahas an object in hand (Moon: pitcher and rosary; Mars: spear; Mercury: bow; Jupiter: pitcher; Rāhu: half of the lunar disc; Ketu: sword and lamp) and no colour. (read, summary of the Gangadharan text: the second URL)

##### 1.5 Vaikhānasasmārtasūtra, Prāśna IV, khaṇḍa 13 (the appeasing of the planets)

Edition opened: W. Caland, *Vaikhānasasmārtasūtram: English translation*, Bibliotheca Indica no. 251, Calcutta 1929 (Sanskrit text by Caland, 1927). (read: https://archive.org/download/Bibliotheca_Indica_Series/VaikhanasaSmartaSutramEnglishTranslation-WCaland1929bis_text.pdf)

Date: Caland argues the Vaikhānasa texts cannot be older than the fourth century CE, partly because the sūtra uses the Greek order of the planets and the week-day names (read: same URL, Introduction §5). David Pingree places the grahaśānti ritual "perhaps" in the fourth or fifth century (read: Pingree, section 1.7).

Caland's rendering of IV.13, in order of the nine grahas Sun, Moon, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn, Rāhu, Ketu (read: same URL):

- Colours: red; white; very red "as the Bandhika flower"; dark blue "as the Karṇikāra"; yellow "as the Kañcana flower"; white "as the Mallikā flower"; black "as the Atasī flower"; black "as collyrium"; black-red. (Caland's "dark blue" for Mercury is odd, since other sources, including T. A. Gopinatha Rao below, call the karṇikāra flower yellow; I did not see the Sanskrit.)
- Presiding deities: Agni, Varuṇa, Rudra, Viṣṇu, Indra, Śacī, Prajāpati, Śeṣa, Yama.
- Stations: middle, south-east, south, north-east, north, east, west, south-west, north-west.
- Pedestals, strewn with rice grains or sand "on his due place": "a square, a circle, a *tutt*-shaped one, a triangle, an octagon, a half-moon-shaped one, a vajra-shaped one, a staff-shaped one, and a banner-shaped one". Caland's footnote explains the third as a square with bent sides, from a commentary note "an oblong maṇḍala made with the middle drawn in, half as wide".

##### 1.6 T. A. Gopinatha Rao, *Elements of Hindu Iconography*, vol. 1 part 2 (Madras, 1914), pp. 304–323

Edition opened: the Law Printing House edition. (read: https://archive.org/details/ZmUE_elements-of-hindu-iconography-vol.-1-part-2-by-t.-a.-gopinath-1914-the-law-printing-house)

Rao gathers the texts of the south Indian temple tradition. What he states, with his authorities (all read, same URL):

- Candra "perfectly white", white garments (*Aṃśumadbhedāgama*), a chariot with ten horses (*Śilparatna*). Bhauma (Mars) red like fire, red garments. Budha yellow like the *karṇikāra* flower, yellow garments (the *Viṣṇudharmottara* instead makes him resemble Viṣṇu). Bṛhaspati golden yellow; Śukra white with white clothing, in a silver chariot (*Viṣṇudharmottara*). Śanaiścara black, with black garments, short and somewhat lame. Ketu "of dark colour".
- A table from the *Rūpamaṇḍana* (a late work on image-making) gives colours: Moon white, Mars red, Mercury yellow, Jupiter yellow, Venus white, Saturn black, Rāhu and Ketu smoke-colour. The scan of the Sun's row is garbled.
- In the Sun temple, "it is said", the other grahas stand: Soma east, Bhauma south-east, Bṛhaspati south, Rāhu south-west, Śukra west, Ketu north-west, Budha north, Śani north-east. Rao observes that this differs from the idea that the nine stand in the order of their zodiacal position when the temple was built.

##### 1.7 David Pingree, "Indian Planetary Images and the Tradition of Astral Magic," *Journal of the Warburg and Courtauld Institutes* 52 (1989), 1–13

Edition opened: the article's offprint, posted as a PDF attachment on the INDOLOGY mailing list. (read: https://list.indology.info/pipermail/indology/attachments/20260321/04ac91b1/attachment.pdf; the message is https://list.indology.info/pipermail/indology/2026-March/061895.html)

What Pingree says (pp. 3–7, 8–10, 12–13), all read in that PDF:

- Colours, directions and substances for the planets entered Indian worship from Sphujidhvaja's *Yavanajātaka* (269/270 CE): Sun coppery red, Moon silver, Mars red, Mercury green, Jupiter yellow, Venus white, Saturn black; and the directions east (Sun, Venus), south (Mars), west (Moon, Saturn), north (Jupiter, Mercury). Mīnarāja's *Vṛddhayavanajātaka* (early fourth century) replaced these with an eight-direction scheme modelled on the eight *dikpālas*: east Sun, south-east Venus, south Mars, south-west Rāhu, west Saturn, north-west Moon, north Mercury, north-east Jupiter. Pingree says this scheme "plays an important role in the ritualistic arrangement of images of the planets in cosmological maṇḍalas".
- Varāhamihira's *Bṛhadyātrā* 18 gives the substances of the images (copper, crystal, red sandalwood, bronze, gold, silver, lead, lead, brass) and "says nothing about their forms". (Pingree's list; core text not reread here.)
- The Vaikhānasasmārtasūtra "added a set of geometrically shaped pedestals": Sun a square, Moon a circle, Mars "shaped like a cardamom (a quadrilateral with curved sides)", Mercury a triangle, Jupiter an octagon, Venus a half-moon, Saturn a diamond, Rāhu a staff, Ketu a banner. The Sun stands in the middle with one of the other eight in each cardinal and intermediate direction, "so that the whole arrangement forms a maṇḍala".
- The Agniveśyagṛhyasūtra has "almost the same" ritual with other shapes: "the Vaikhānasas' square pedestal for the Sun and circular for the Moon are interchanged, Mars's becomes a triangle, Mercury's is shaped like an arrow, Jupiter's is an oblong, Venus's a pentagon, Saturn's takes on the form of a bow and Rāhu's that of a winnowing basket, while Ketu's retains the shape of a banner." Pingree's conclusion on this point is that "variation with regard to the forms of the pedestals was quite normal within the Indian tradition".
- Bhuvanadeva's *Aparājitapṛcchā* (an architecture treatise, late twelfth century, ch. 214) gives mounts: Sun seven horses, Moon ten, Mars ram, Mercury peacock, Jupiter goose, Venus frog, Saturn buffalo, Rāhu in the middle of a pot (Pingree p. 7; I did not open the text).
- The Buddhist *Niṣpannayogāvalī* (early twelfth century, Abhayākaragupta), in its Dharmadhātuvāgīśvara maṇḍala, gives colours: Sun red, Moon white, Mars red, Mercury yellow, Jupiter yellow, Venus white, Saturn black, Rāhu red-black, Ketu black (Pingree's translation, p. 7).
- Pingree compares the shapes of the bases of the planetary temples at Harran (Syria), as reported by al-Masʿūdī and al-Dimashqī, with the Vaikhānasa pedestals, and suggests as a hypothesis that the Harranian idea came from the Vaiṣṇava pedestals. Both Arab authors give the Sun a square base; the others vary (Moon octagon or pentagon, Saturn hexagon, Jupiter triangle, Mars oblong, Venus and Mercury compound figures) (pp. 9–10). Harranian priests wore each planet's colour, as the Vaiṣṇavas clothe each image in its colour (p. 10).

##### 1.8 The 2026 INDOLOGY thread "Symbolic Forms of the Navagrahas", and Marianna Kropf's table

Message of 18 March 2026 (Jacob Østergaard): asks where the symbolic forms (*ākāra*) of the nine grahas come from, and lists those he found: Sun circle (*vṛtta*), Moon crescent (*ardhacandra*), Mars triangle (*trikoṇa*), Mercury arrowhead (*bāṇa*), Jupiter rectangle (*dīrghacaturaśra*), Venus pentagon (*pañcakoṇa*), Saturn bow (*dhanus*), Rāhu winnowing basket (*śūrpa*), Ketu flag (*dhvaja*). (read, summary: https://list.indology.info/pipermail/indology/2026-March/061848.html)

Replies, in short (read, summary of each page: https://list.indology.info/pipermail/indology/2026-March/061856.html, https://list.indology.info/pipermail/indology/2026-March/061864.html, https://list.indology.info/pipermail/indology/2026-March/061873.html, https://list.indology.info/pipermail/indology/2026-March/061895.html):

- Martin Gansten: he does not expect the shapes in astrological texts and points to ritual manuals and texts on temporary maṇḍalas and to S. K. Ramachandra Rao's *Navagraha-Kośa*; he reads Rāhu's basket as a stylised severed head and wonders whether Venus's shape comes from its eight-year cycle with the Sun.
- Østergaard: he meets the shapes on gameboards, playing pieces and *ganjifa* cards from early to mid nineteenth-century Mysore, and in a Tamil *pañcāṅga* image (shared by Jean-Luc Chevillard). Dominik Wujastyk points to Pingree (1989) p. 5, quoted above.
- Sudha Berry: points to R. C. Gupta, "Mystical Mathematics of Ancient Planets," *Indian Journal of History of Science* 40 (2005). A search snippet of the paper says planets were represented by the square, circle, semicircle, triangle, pentagon, a "bow-figure" and the lotus, and that a nine-celled square graha-maṇḍala is attributed to a Parāśara work (search only; I could not open the paper: https://cahc.jainuniversity.ac.in/assets/ijhs/Vol1_2005_03_MYSTICAL MATHEMATICS IN ANCIENT PLANETS.pdf).

Kropf, *Rituelle Traditionen der Planetengottheiten (Navagraha) im Kathmandutal* (Heidelberg dissertation, 2005), pp. 195–196, attached to a list message of 20 March 2026. (read: https://list.indology.info/pipermail/indology/attachments/20260320/d9c10eae/attachment.pdf)

- p. 195: the earliest sketch of ritual representations is YājS 1.297–298a (metal images, or a maṇḍala); "since the earliest descriptions the colours specific to each graha have kept a central importance"; they are built into the maṇḍalas and into the flowers, cloths and gifts, "to this day".
- p. 196: a table headed "the iconographic details of the navagrahas in classical texts (YājS, JaiGS, MaP)". I reproduce it as printed, in German terms translated. (JaiGS is the *Jaiminigṛhyasūtra*; I did not open it.) Kropf does not say in the passage I have which of the three texts gives which cell; her n. 45 says the YājS gives no form or direction.

| Graha | Colour | Symbol | Position | Mount |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Sūrya | red | circle | centre | chariot with 7 horses / peacock |
| Candra | white | square or crescent | south | chariot with 10 horses |
| Maṅgala | green (MaP: yellowish) | arrow | north-east | goose or swan |
| Budha | red | triangle | north | horse |
| Bṛhaspati | yellow | rectangle | east | elephant |
| Śukra | white | pentagon or six-pointed star | south-east | Garuḍa or swan |
| Śani | black (MaP: dark) | bow or snake | west | crow or raven |
| Rāhu | black (MaP: dark) | winnowing basket / trapezium | south-west | goat |
| Ketu | variegated (MaP: smoky) | flag | north-west | lion |

The Maṅgala and Budha rows look exchanged in the printed table: green and "arrow" are Mercury's elsewhere, red and "triangle" Mars's. I cannot tell whether this is a misprint or a source reading. Kropf's n. 46 names two surveys of the textual passages on graha images: Markel (1995, pp. 183–194) and Yano (1998) for the Purāṇas, and lists MaP 94.1–9, AgniP 51, VdhP 3.67–69, NāP 55.97–100, SkaP 2.9; 27.88–99 (read: same PDF). I opened neither survey.

##### 1.9 Viṣṇudharmottarapurāṇa, part III, chapter 69 (images of the planets)

Edition opened: Stella Kramrisch, *The Vishnudharmottara (Part III): A Treatise on Indian Painting and Image-Making*, 2nd revised and enlarged edition, Calcutta University Press, 1928. Her numbering is "Part III, Ch. 69"; Kropf cites the same passage as 3.67–69 in another edition. (read: https://archive.org/details/vishnudharmottar031493mbp)

Date: Kramrisch's introduction argues the text was compiled around the seventh century (read: same URL).

- Mars to be made "like Agni" on a golden chariot with eight horses; Mercury "like Viṣṇu" on a similar chariot; Jupiter "of the colour of heated gold", two hands with a book and rosary, yellow garment; Venus "a white body wearing a white garment", two hands with a jewel receptacle and a book; Saturn "dark and wearing a dark garment", with "extended veins", on an iron chariot drawn by eight serpents; Rāhu on a silver car, "only the head" with one hand; Ketu made like Mars with ten horses. (read: same URL)

#### 2. Vāstu: site, plan, proportion and direction

##### 2.1 Bṛhatsaṃhitā, the chapter on house-building (*vāstuvidyā*)

Edition opened: N. Chidambaram Iyer's 1884 English translation as hosted on Wisdomlib, where it is chapter 53; Kramrisch cites the same chapter as LII in the Sanskrit editions (numbering differs by edition). (read: https://www.wisdomlib.org/hinduism/book/brihat-samhita/d/doc229297.html)

Date: sixth century CE (memory).

- v. 96: "If the ground be white, red, yellow or black, or if it be found to smell like ghee, blood, cooked rice or liquor, the Brāhmaṇas, the Kṣatriyas, the Vaiśyas or the Śūdras will respectively prosper." (read)
- v. 97: grasses and tastes (sweet, astringent, sour, pungent) tell the same four classes apart in the same order. (read)
- v. 95: a flower of the class's colour buried in the pit must neither fade nor change colour; v. 70 and 91: the Brāhmaṇas and other classes dwell on the northern, eastern, southern and western sides respectively, and the matching class suffers if its side is low. (read)
- v. 42–52: the ground is divided into 81 squares by ten lines each way, with 45 deities; Brahmā holds the nine central squares; the Vāstupuruṣa (the demon laid in the plan) lies face down with his head in the north-east. The text assigns deities, not planets, to the squares. (read)
- v. 115: "if the ground be of irregular shape, kinsmen will die; if the sides are irregular, there will be no pregnancy." (read)
- v. 118: the shrine of the gods in the north-east, the kitchen south-east, utensils south-west, treasury and granary north-west. (read)

##### 2.2 Mayamata, chapter II ("the kinds of site") and chapter XV (pillars)

Edition opened: Bruno Dagens, *Mayamata: traité sanskrit d'architecture*, Pondicherry, Institut français d'Indologie (French translation with Sanskrit text); the scan's recognition is partial. (read: https://archive.org/details/mayamatabronodagens_202002)

Date: not established in what I read.

- II.10–15a, in my English from the French: the site suited to Brāhmaṇas is square, white, spotless, planted with *udumbara* (cluster fig) trees, sloping to the north, astringent and sweet in taste. The site for princes is longer than wide by an eighth, red, pungent, sloping east, planted with *aśvattha* (sacred fig). For Vaiśyas, longer than wide by a sixth, sloping east, yellow, sour, planted with *plakṣa*. For Śūdras, longer than wide by a quarter, sloping east, black, planted with *nyagrodha* (banyan); the word for its taste is lost in the scan. (read, French)
- XV.13–16: a pillar square at the foot with bulb and abacus is *brahmakānta*; an octagonal one *viṣṇukānta*; with bulb and abacus and perfectly circular, *rudrakānta*; the scan's names for the hexagonal and sixteen-sided pillars are damaged. (read, French; OCR damaged)
- Kramrisch quotes Mayamata III.1: "The shape of the Vastu for gods and Brahmanas is prescribed as square." (read: Kramrisch, 2.5 below)

##### 2.3 Mānasāra (translation by Prasanna Kumar Acharya, 1934)

Edition opened: *Architecture of Manasara*, translated from the Sanskrit, Manasara Series vol. IV (Munshiram Manoharlal reprint of the 1934 Oxford edition). (read: https://archive.org/details/in.ernet.dli.2015.31241)

Date: not given in the passages I read.

- III.18–29, sites for the four classes: for Brāhmaṇas, "square, whitish in colour", fig tree, sloping north, "sweet and fragrant in taste"; for Kṣatriyas, length exceeding breadth by one-eighth, reddish, sloping east, bitter, peepal tree; for Vaiśyas, one-sixth longer, yellowish, sloping east, sour; for Śūdras, one-fourth longer, black, sloping east, pungent, banyan. The "first two" are called best and the last two "fair". (read)
- IV.11–12: ground "white, red, golden, black or grey in colour, and hexagonal (in shape)" brings all prosperity. (read)
- XXX.168–194, "Āya and other formulas" (the six measurement checks): a building's dimensions are multiplied and divided to give remainders which name an *āya* (gain, a group of twelve), *vyaya* (loss, ten), *yoni* (source, eight), *nakṣatra* (lunar mansion, twenty-seven; Acharya glosses it "planet"), *vāra* (day, seven "days starting with Sunday"), *aṃśa* (part, nine) and *tithi* (lunar day, fifteen). Verses 183–184: the circumference or height is multiplied by six, eight or nine and divided by seven, and the remainder is the *vāra*. Verse 190: "Prosperity would take leave of a building if its measurements be not verified by the whole set of six formulas." The seven weekday rulers (grahas) thus act as a remainder test on dimensions. (read)

##### 2.4 Site shapes and colours by class, side by side

| Class | Bṛhatsaṃhitā (Iyer) | Mayamata II | Mānasāra III |
|---|---|---|---|
| Brāhmaṇa | white; smell of ghee; sweet taste | square; white; astringent and sweet; slopes N; udumbara | square; whitish; sweet, fragrant; slopes N; fig |
| Kṣatriya | red; smell of blood; astringent | 1/8 longer; red; pungent; slopes E; aśvattha | 1/8 longer; reddish; bitter; slopes E; peepal |
| Vaiśya | yellow; smell of cooked rice; sour | 1/6 longer; yellow; sour; slopes E; plakṣa | 1/6 longer; yellowish; sour; slopes E; fig |
| Śūdra | black; smell of liquor; pungent | 1/4 longer; black; taste lost; slopes E; nyagrodha | 1/4 longer; black; pungent; slopes E; banyan |

Marks: all three columns read (2.1–2.3). The Kṣatriya taste is astringent, pungent and bitter in the three texts as translated.

##### 2.5 Stella Kramrisch, *The Hindu Temple* (Calcutta, 1946), vol. 1

Edition opened: University of Calcutta, 1946 (OCR of the scan; page numbers below are the printed ones as I could read them, and approximate). (read: https://archive.org/details/in.ernet.dli.2015.62118)

- Vāstupuruṣamaṇḍala: its form "is a square. This is its essential form. It can be converted into a triangle, hexagon, octagon and circle of equal area and retain its symbolism" (she cites the Bṛhatsaṃhitā, chapter LII, verse 56, with its commentary). (read, early in vol. 1, the chapter on the Vāstupuruṣamaṇḍala)
- "The square is the mark of order, of finality to the expanding life ... The circle and curve belong to life in its growth and movement." She cites the Vedic fire altars: the square Āhavanīya hearth for heaven, the circular Gārhapatya for the terrestrial world, the Dakṣiṇāgni for the air-world (from the Śatapathabrāhmaṇa and the Śulbasūtras). (read, same place)
- The colour of building material by class: in the *Viṣṇudharmottara*, part III, ch. XC.2, white stones for Brāhmaṇas, red for Kṣatriyas, yellow for Vaiśyas and black for Śūdras, "in exact imitation of the colour of the soil"; white clay for the bricks of Brāhmaṇas "and so on" (ch. XCI.1). (read, about pp. 112–113)
- On the guṇas in the temple, see section 4.

#### 3. Colour, rasa and image-making: Nāṭyaśāstra, Viṣṇudharmottara, Śilparatna

##### 3.1 Nāṭyaśāstra VI.42–45 (rasa, colour, presiding deity)

Edition opened: the Sanskrit and English of the siva.sh electronic edition of chapter 6, verses 41–45. (read: https://www.siva.sh/natyashastra/6/1/41-45)

Date: not given on the page; the text is early (memory).

- 6.42: *śyāmo bhavati śṛṅgāraḥ sito hāsyaḥ prakīrtitaḥ / kapotaḥ karuṇaś caiva rakto raudraḥ prakīrtitaḥ.* The erotic (*śṛṅgāra*) is dark (*śyāma*); the comic (*hāsya*) white; the pathetic (*karuṇa*) grey-dove (*kapota*); the furious (*raudra*) red.
- 6.43: *gauro vīras tu vijñeyaḥ kṛṣṇaś caiva bhayānakaḥ / nīlavarṇas tu bībhatsaḥ pītaś caivādbhutaḥ smṛtaḥ.* The heroic (*vīra*) fair or golden (*gaura*); the fearful (*bhayānaka*) black; the odious (*bībhatsa*) blue; the marvellous (*adbhuta*) yellow.
- 6.44–45: deities: Viṣṇu for *śṛṅgāra*, the Pramathas for *hāsya*, Rudra for *raudra*, Yama for *karuṇa*, Mahākāla for *bībhatsa*, Kāla for *bhayānaka*, Mahendra for *vīra*, Brahmā for *adbhuta*. (all read, same URL)
- Eight rasas appear here; there is no colour for *śānta* (peace), the ninth rasa that later writers add.

##### 3.2 Nāṭyaśāstra XXIII (costume and make-up)

Edition opened: the English translation on Wisdomlib (the footnote style suggests Manomohan Ghosh's; the page does not name the translator), chapter XXIII, verses 72–103. (read: https://www.wisdomlib.org/hinduism/book/the-natyashastra/d/doc210153.html)

- 73–74 (the page prints the first as "75"): "the four original colours are black, blue, yellow and red" as the page prints it; the derivative colours that follow are made from white, so "black" appears to be a slip for white. (read; flagged)
- 75–77: derivative colours: *kāraṇḍava* (bluish white) from white and blue; *pāṇḍu* (yellowish white) from white and yellow; *padma* (lotus) from white and red; *harit* (green) from yellow and blue; *kāṣāya* (dark red) from blue and red; *gaura* (pale red) from red and yellow; blue is "the strongest of colours" (verses 79–80). (read)
- 90: "Gods as well as the Yakṣas and the Apsarasas should be painted reddish yellow, and Rudra, Arka (the Sun), Druhiṇa (Brahmā) and Skanda are to have the colour of gold." 91: "Soma (the Moon), Bṛhaspati, Śakra (Indra), Varuṇa and the stars, the ocean, the Himalayas, and Gaṅgā are to be made white." 92: "Aṅgāraka (Maṅgala = Mars) should be painted red, and Budha and Hutāśana (Agni) yellow, and Nārāyaṇa and Nara as well as Vāsuki should be dark blue (*śyāma*)." 93: Daityas, Dānavas, Rākṣasas, mountains, Piśācas, Yama and the sky are dark blue. (read)

##### 3.3 Viṣṇudharmottarapurāṇa, part III, painting chapters (Kramrisch's translation of 1928)

Edition: see 1.9. (read: https://archive.org/details/vishnudharmottar031493mbp)

- Ch. 27 verses 7–26 (colours): "there are five principal colours, viz., white (*śveta*), red (*rakta*), yellow (*pīta*), black (*kṛṣṇa*) and green (*harit*)"; white has five shades and the dark (*śyāma*) twelve. Deities not named are painted white; the Nāga Vāsuki *śyāma*; the twice-born "the colour of the moon", Kṣatriyas "of the colour of the *padma* (white lotus)", Vaiśyas slightly light, Śūdras dark; "those who are oppressed by evil stars ... should also be dark." (read)
- A second list further on (the colouring chapter) gives the five primaries as white, yellow, "the colour of the myrobalan", black and blue. (read) Kramrisch notes that ch. 27 "is borrowed verbatim from Bharata's Nāṭyaśāstra" and that the *Viṣṇudharmottara* counts nine rasas where Bharata deals with eight (read: her introduction). The lists in ch. 27, in the colouring chapter and in the Nāṭyaśāstra (four primaries) differ.
- Ch. 43 verses 1–39: nine rasas in painting: *śṛṅgāra*, *hāsya*, *karuṇa*, *vīra*, *raudra*, *bhayānaka*, *bībhatsa*, *adbhuta*, *śānta*; domestic pictures should be of *śṛṅgāra*, *hāsya* and *śānta*; "in the place of a ruler and in the temple of a god all the sentiments may be represented". This chapter as Kramrisch translates it carries no colour per rasa. (read)
- Image-making chapters (Kramrisch's numbering; the heading of the first is damaged in the scan, about ch. 44): Brahmā's form, from rajas, "the colour is reddish"; Viṣṇu's from sattva; Rudra's from tamas. Ch. 47: "*vidyā* (learning) is pointed out as white, and *ajñāna* ... is said to be black." Ch. 52: Yama, tamas-predominant, is "described as resembling in colour the blue-lotus". (read)

##### 3.4 The rasa-colour list in Kramrisch's introduction and its sources

Kramrisch's introduction (p. 19) says "Each rasa (emotion) had to be painted in its expressive colour": *śṛṅgāra* of *śyāma* hue, *hāsya* white, *karuṇa* grey, *raudra* red, *vīra* yellowish white, *bhayānaka* black, the marvellous yellow, the repulsive blue, with notes to the *Śilparatna* verses 145–147 and the *Nāṭyaśāstra* VI.42–43. She also writes that statues, "according to their Sāttvika, rājasika and tāmasika aspects, had to be painted white, red or dark", without a verse reference. (read: same URL; her p. 18–19)

I looked in the *Śilparatna* (Śrīkumāra, attributed to the sixteenth century: search only, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shilparatna; Trivandrum Sanskrit Series vols. 75 and 98, https://archive.org/details/TSS075TheSilparatnaOfSriKumaraKSambasivaSastriVolume11922_201809), but the scan is too damaged to read its painting chapter (46), so I cannot confirm her statement from the text.

##### 3.5 Rasa colours side by side

Marks: Nāṭyaśāstra from 3.1 (read); Kramrisch's list from 3.4 (read).

| Rasa | Nāṭyaśāstra VI.42–43 | Kramrisch's introduction |
|---|---|---|
| śṛṅgāra (erotic) | śyāma (dark) | śyāma hue |
| hāsya (comic) | sita (white) | white |
| karuṇa (pathetic) | kapota (dove-grey) | grey |
| raudra (furious) | rakta (red) | red |
| vīra (heroic) | gaura (fair, pale gold) | yellowish white |
| bhayānaka (fearful) | kṛṣṇa (black) | black |
| bībhatsa (odious) | nīla (blue) | blue |
| adbhuta (marvellous) | pīta (yellow) | yellow |
| śānta (peace) | none | none |

#### 4. The guṇas and colour, form and architecture

- Kramrisch, *The Hindu Temple* (read: https://archive.org/details/in.ernet.dli.2015.62118): "The 3 Guṇas are Sattva, Rajas and Tamas. Their colours are white, red and black, and their inherent tendencies are ascending, expanding and descending, respectively" (a footnote near printed p. 122). White plastered temples show "their 'sāttvika' quality", "the ascending tendency within the sattva guṇa" expressed in the vertical of the tower; the dark sunk sanctum (*garbhagṛha*, womb-chamber) is the place of tamas, "the descending tendency"; the tower widens "as far as rajas"; the three guṇas "have in the form of the temple a comprehensive visual symbol" (near p. 172). She cites no verse.
- *Viṣṇudharmottara* part III (read: 3.3): Brahmā from rajas, reddish; Viṣṇu from sattva; Rudra from tamas; Yama's tamas, blue-lotus colour.
- Gheraṇḍasaṃhitā III.77 (Vasu's translation): the air tattva is "full of Satva quality". (read: https://archive.org/details/55915gherandasamhitabyscvasu)
- B. V. Raman and Frawley assign the guṇas to grahas (section 5). Both give the same division as the classical texts (memory: it is Parāśara's).

---

#### 5. Modern jyotiṣa authors

##### 5.1 B. V. Raman, *Hindu Predictive Astrology*

Edition opened: a scan whose listing gives 1996; publisher and edition number are not on the page I read. (read: https://archive.org/details/hindu-predictive-astrology-b.-v.-raman)

- Chapter "Astrological Terminology" (printed pp. 19–20), the passage headed "Colours": "Each planet represents a certain colour which is as follows: Sun—copper, Mars—blood red, Moon—white, Saturn—black, Mercury—green, Jupiter—bright yellow and Venus—mixture of all colours." Rāhu and Ketu are not coloured. These agree with the colours Pingree attributes to Sphujidhvaja for Sun, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter and Saturn; they differ for the Moon (silver there, white here) and Venus (white there) (read; the comparison is mine from two read lists).
- Same chapter: "The Sun, the Moon and Jupiter are divine in nature and indicate Satvikaguna ... Venus and Mercury represent Rajasa ... Mars and Saturn denote Thamasa." And elements: "The Sun and Mars are fiery planets; Mercury is earthy; Saturn is airy; Jupiter is ethereal; and Venus and the Moon are watery." (read)
- Chapter XXIII, "Characteristics of the Signs and Planets" (printed pp. 135–139), written for horary questions on lost things. Under the signs, places: Aries lands of sheep and goats, caves, mines; Gemini musical halls, theatres, libraries, granaries; Leo forts, palaces; Libra roads, streets, commercial places; Scorpio kitchens, vaults, underground cells; Sagittarius armouries, stables, sacrificial places; Capricorn temple precincts, tombs; Pisces shrines, altars, hermitages; and each sign owns a limb of the "Kālapuruṣa" (Time-Man), Aries the head to Pisces the feet. (read)
- Same chapter, under the planets: the Sun "temples and places of worship ... sacrificial places, coronation chambers"; Moon "bath-rooms, springs, watery places"; Mars "southern directions ... fire places, cook-rooms, chemical laboratories, fortifications"; Mercury "architecture ... northern direction ... green colour"; Jupiter "north-eastern direction, shrines, temples"; Venus "bedrooms ... south-eastern direction ... variegated colours"; Saturn "architectural skill ... houses ... western direction". (read)

##### 5.2 David Frawley, *The Astrology of the Seers*

Edition opened: the Motilal Banarsidass edition (Delhi, based on the 1990 first edition; the listing gives 1990). (read: https://archive.org/details/astrology-of-the-seers-david-frawley)

- pp. 69–70: Sattva for the Sun, Moon and Jupiter; Rajas for Mercury and Venus; Tamas for Mars and Saturn, "also Rahu and Ketu". (read)
- Chapter on treatment methods (pp. ~240–255): a colour for strengthening each planet: Sun red, yellow, gold, orange; Moon white; Mars red and fiery in darker hues, and black; Mercury emerald or green; Jupiter yellow, orange, gold; Venus "all colours, particularly a rainbow-like effect"; Saturn dark blue; Rāhu like Jupiter's and the Sun's; Ketu fiery red, orange, yellow. (read)
- pp. 261–263, "Forms of planetary deities": a verse for each with colour and mount: Sun "lustre of a red lotus", seven-headed horse; Moon white, deer; Mars red, ram; Mercury green, a lion with an elephant's trunk; Jupiter yellow, elephant; Venus white, white horse; Saturn dark blue, ox; Rāhu "dressed in black, black in color", a lion; Ketu "dressed in grey, of the color of smoke", a serpent. A table lists presiding deities and mounts, ending with Rāhu a lion and Ketu a serpent. (read)

##### 5.3 Sanjay Rath

I searched the text of *Crux of Vedic Astrology: Timing of Events* (1998 scan) for "colour", "shape", "yantra" and "mandala" and found only incidental uses (a yellow two-armed form of the Divine Mother; "the color of dress" as a sign matter), no graha-colour or shape table. (read: https://archive.org/details/sanjayrathcruxofvedicastrologytimingofevents19982)

#### 6. The five mahābhūtas, their shapes and colours, in Tantra and haṭha texts

##### 6.1 Gheraṇḍasaṃhitā, chapter III (the five *dhāraṇā*, holdings of the breath in an element)

Date: the Kaivalyadhama edition's introduction gives the seventeenth century or the beginning of the eighteenth (the passage in my scan is brief). (read: https://archive.org/details/gheranda-samhita_202506)

Two editions differ in wording. Vasu's English translation, Lesson III, verses 70–80 (read: https://archive.org/details/55915gherandasamhitabyscvasu):

- Earth (*pārthivī*): colour of orpiment (yellow), "its form is four-sided", seed *laṃ*, Brahmā; kept in the heart for five *ghaṭikās* (two hours).
- Water (*āmbhasī*): "white like the Kunda-flower or a conch or the moon, its form is circular like the moon", seed *vaṃ*, Viṣṇu.
- Fire (*āgneyī*): "red like the Indra-gopa insect, its form is triangular", seed *raṃ*, Rudra; at the navel.
- Air (*vāyavī*): "black as unguent for the eyes", seed *yaṃ*, Īśvara; "full of Satva quality".
- Ether (*ākāśī*): "the colour of pure sea-water", seed *haṃ*, Sadāśiva.

The Kaivalyadhama edition (Lonavla), verses 3.59–63, Sanskrit as read from a scan with damaged print (read: https://archive.org/details/gheranda-samhita_202506): earth *haritāladeśaracitaṃ bhaumaṃ lakārānvitaṃ ... caturasraṃ* (four-sided); water *śaṅkhendupratimaṃ kundadhavalaṃ ... vakāra bīja ... viṣṇunā* (no shape stated); fire *indragopasadṛśaṃ bījaṃ trikoṇānvitaṃ ... rudreṇa* (triangle); air *dhūmrāvabhāsaṃ ... sattvamayaṃ yakārasahitaṃ ... īśvaro devatā* (smoky); ether *varaśuddhavārisadṛśaṃ vyomākhyaṃ ... sadāśivena ... hakārānvitam*. The two editions agree on earth four-sided and fire triangular; "circular like the moon" for water is in Vasu and I did not see it here; air is "black" in Vasu and "smoky" here.

##### 6.2 Śivasvarodaya (Ram Kumar Rai's text with English translation, Prachya Prakashan, Varanasi)

Edition opened: Rai's text with English translation and notes. Date not given in what I read; the work is medieval (memory). (read: https://archive.org/details/yshivasvarodayatextwithenglishtranslationramkumarrai)

- The tattva shown by the breath (*svara*): earth "yellow, square in shape, sweet in taste", twelve finger-breadths long; water "white, like a half-moon", sixteen; fire "red and triangular", pungent, four; air "blue in colour" (another verse: "dark"), "round" in the commentary, sour, eight; ether "an assortment of all other Svaras".
- Seed syllables as shapes to meditate on: *laṃ* in a square, yellow, shining like gold; *vaṃ* half-moon shaped; *raṃ* triangular, red; *yaṃ* round, black; *haṃ* formless, extra bright.
- A test: breathe on a mirror and read the damp patch: square for earth, half-moon for water, triangular for fire, round for air, dots or tiny drops for ether. Pressing the eyes with the fingers shows colours: yellow for earth, red for fire, black for air, a spotted colour for ether (the colour for water is lost in my scan). (all read)

##### 6.3 Ṣaṭcakranirūpaṇa (the "Description of the Six Centres"), in Arthur Avalon (Sir John Woodroffe), *The Serpent Power*

Edition opened: *The Serpent Power*, Ganesh & Co. (the scan on archive.org). (read: https://archive.org/details/dli.ministry.26996)

Woodroffe's table (his page "The Centres or Lotuses") gives, centre by centre, element, colour and the shape of its maṇḍala: *mūlādhāra* earth, yellow, square; *svādhiṣṭhāna* water, white, crescent; *maṇipūra* fire, red, triangle; *anāhata* air, smoky, six-pointed hexagon; *viśuddha* ether, white, circle. His notes: the earth maṇḍala is "square ... surrounded by eight shining spears" (verse 5); the water maṇḍala is "in the shape of a crescent moon" (verses 14–18); the heart lotus has a hexagonal air maṇḍala "of a smoky colour" (summary of verses 22–27); in the throat lotus "the Ethereal Region is circular in shape". His footnote says the element diagrams are also given in *Śāradātilaka* 1 and the *Viśvasāra Tantra*, except that "according to the Viśvasāra Tantra, the Maṇḍala of water is not a crescent, but eight-cornered (Aṣṭāsra)", and "different Tantras give different descriptions". (read)

##### 6.4 Śāradātilaka, chapter 1 (verses 22–25, with the commentary of Rāghavabhaṭṭa)

Edition opened: the Śāradātilaka of Lakṣmaṇadeśikendra with the *Padārthādarśa* commentary, a scan whose Devanagari recognition is poor. (read, damaged: https://archive.org/details/zqft_saradatilaka-of-shri-lakshmana-deshikendra-with-padarthadarsha-commentary-by-shr)

Date: probably twelfth century per the abstract of Bühnemann's article (search only: https://cambridge.org/core/journals/bulletin-of-the-school-of-oriental-and-african-studies/article/saradatilakatantra-on-yoga-a-new-edition-and-translation-of-chapter-25/65682490D8D1EFE256AEEE5655736EC4).

In the commentary on verses 23–24 ("*bhūtamaṇḍalāny āha*", "he now tells the maṇḍalas of the elements") I could read fragments: sky a circle (*vṛtta*); air "marked with six dots" (*ṣaḍbindulāñchita*); fire a triangle apex upward (*trikoṇam ūrdhvāgram*) with a svastika; water a half-moon with a lotus (*ardhendusaṃyuta*, *ambhoja*); earth *caturasraṃ savajrakam* (a square with *vajra*); and the maṇḍalas are to be filled with powders (*rajas*) of the several colours. I cannot give clean verses from this scan.

##### 6.5 The mahābhūta shapes side by side

| Element | Gheraṇḍa (Vasu) | Gheraṇḍa (Kaivalyadhama) | Śivasvarodaya | Ṣaṭcakranirūpaṇa (Woodroffe) | Śāradātilaka commentary |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Earth | yellow; four-sided | yellow; four-sided | yellow; square | yellow; square | square with vajra |
| Water | white; circular | white; no shape | white; half-moon | white; crescent (Viśvasāra: eight-cornered) | half-moon with lotus |
| Fire | red; triangular | red; triangle | red; triangle | red; triangle | triangle, apex up, svastika |
| Air | black; no shape | smoky; no shape | blue or dark; round | smoky; six-pointed hexagon | six dots |
| Ether | sea-water colour; no shape | sea-water colour; no shape | all colours, formless, dots | white; circle | circle |
