#### 7. Indian art theory

- Stella Kramrisch: see 2.5 (square and circle), 3.4 (rasa colours) and 4 (guṇas, white, red and black, and the temple). (read)
- Ananda Coomaraswamy, *The Transformation of Nature in Art* (1934): I searched the text for guṇa, colour and rasa. It treats rasa as "the specific rasas or emotional colorings" of a work, quotes a text in which viewers of a painting of Jinas (Jain saints) "talk of the colors, white, black, yellow, blue, and red", and compares the Six Canons of Chinese painting with the Indian theory. I found no graha, rāśi or guṇa mapped to a shape or colour in it. (read: https://archive.org/details/dli.ernet.6269)
- Abanindranath Tagore (*Ṣaḍaṅga*, 1921, on the six limbs of painting, taken from Yaśodhara's commentary on the *Kāmasūtra*) and Abhinavagupta (*Abhinavabhāratī*, on rasa and *śānta*): a search found the titles and the topics, with no passage tying either to graha, rāśi or guṇa. (search only: https://catalog.crl.edu/Record/8089336d-a42c-51cd-a8a2-5ca51a57a74f/Description?lng=es; https://sahapedia.org/abhinavagupta)

---

#### 8. Kolam (Tamil Nadu), rangoli and related floor drawing

- A Sahapedia article on kōlam lists circle, triangle, square and spiral as basic shapes; designs built on dot grids (*pulli*) in square, triangle, rectangle or rhombic arrays; rice flour as the main medium, with coloured powders; *kāvi*, a brick-red powder, as border; yellow (turmeric) in marriage designs; the six-pointed star made of opposed triangles as "the union of male and female"; "navagraha kōlam" made "only in front of household shrines, and only on special occasions"; and, citing Gift Siromoney, some yantra or Tantric designs in kōlam "of quite ancient origin". It gives no shapes for planets. (read, summary: https://www.sahapedia.org/significance-of-kolam-tamil-culture)
- Where the texts above mention such floor drawing: *Matsyapurāṇa* 93 (planets drawn "with uncooked rice powder"), *Yājñavalkyasmṛti* 1.298 and *Agnipurāṇa* 164 (maṇḍalas with scented pastes), the *Śāradātilaka* commentary (maṇḍalas filled with coloured powders), the Vaikhānasa sūtra (pedestal shapes strewn in rice grains or sand). (read: sections 1.1, 1.2, 1.4, 1.5, 6.4)

---

#### 9. What I could not find or open

- A text, opened and read by me, that gives a shape for each of the nine grahas in verse. The pedestal shapes reach me through Caland's translation of the Vaikhānasa sūtra and through Pingree's account of the Agniveśya sūtra; I did not open the *Agniveśyagṛhyasūtra* or the *Jaiminigṛhyasūtra*. Where Kropf's table takes its shapes from I could not determine; her note says the YājS gives none.
- Kropf's pp. 195–196 and Pingree's article reached me only as attachments to mailing-list posts. The rest of Kropf's chapter 6 and her appendix IV on materials ("Material-Tabellen für die Navagraha") I did not see. Markel (1995) and Yano (1998), the two surveys she names, I did not open.
- S. K. Ramachandra Rao, *Navagraha-Kośa*: not opened. R. C. Gupta's 2005 paper: its address returned 404; I have only a search snippet.
- The *Aparājitapṛcchā* (chapter 214, planet images) and the *Vāstusāraprakaraṇa* appendix: known to me only from Pingree. The *Samarāṅgaṇasūtradhāra*: I downloaded a scan but its Devanagari is too damaged to quote; a Wisdomlib summary I opened did not give its site-selection colours. The *Śilparatna* painting chapter (ch. 46): same problem. The *Mānasollāsa* painting chapter: a search summary says it counts four pure colours (white, red, yellow, black) and names *bhāvacitra* and *dhūlicitra* pictures; I did not open it (search only: https://www.wisdomlib.org/hinduism/essay/manasollasa-study-of-arts-and-sciences/d/doc1474602.html). The *Śrītattvanidhi*: not located.
- The Sanskrit of the *Mayamata* (I read only Dagens's French); the Sanskrit of *Matsyapurāṇa* 93.17 (only through a summarising page); clean verses of *Śāradātilaka* 1 and the *Viśvasāra Tantra*.
- Kramrisch's source for the white-red-dark painting of statues by guṇa (3.4) and for the colours of the guṇas (4): she cites none in the passages I read.
- Other books by B. V. Raman, K. N. Rao, Hart de Fouw and Robert Svoboda's *Light on Life* (the scan is lent only; a search returned nothing on its colour tables), Rath's *Vedic Remedies in Astrology*: not read.
- Passages assigning a colour, shape or proportion to each of the twelve rāśis in architecture or painting: apart from *Agnipurāṇa* 51 (twelve-petalled Sun diagram with twelve coloured śaktis) and Raman's places by sign, I found none. Pingree says (p. 2, read) that Sphujidhvaja's verbal images of the signs became the traditional Indian representations of the twelve rāśis; I did not trace them.
- Any text I opened that ties one guṇa to one graha, rāśi or mahābhūta by shape; the one guṇa-to-form statement I found is Kramrisch's reading of the temple (section 4). The guṇa column of Gheraṇḍa is a single phrase (air "full of sattva").
- Pages that refused access: vedapath.app (HTTP 403, for *Matsyapurāṇa* 93–94 and *Agnipurāṇa* chapters) and sacred-texts.com (HTTP 403, for the *Gheraṇḍasaṃhitā*).

## Part Three: Measured or widely held outside astrology

This part is kept apart from Parts One and Two: it is not astrology.

### What is measured, or widely held, about the feeling of a colour, a shape, a line weight and a size

This part collects studies from experimental psychology, perception science and design research. It gathers; it draws no conclusions of its own.

#### How to read the marks

- **(read: URL)**: I opened that page, abstract page or full text, and the claim is taken from it. Where the source was a PDF, I extracted its text and read it.
- **(summary only: URL)**: the claim appears only in a search-result summary of that page, which I did not open myself. Treat it as unconfirmed.
- **(memory)**: I recall it and have no page in hand. Not verified.

A bare "abstract" means I read the abstract and not the full paper, so the numbers are only those the abstract gives. Europe PMC (europepmc.org) was used as a mirror of the PubMed abstract where PubMed itself refused automated access; the DOI is the stable identifier.

Effect sizes: Cohen's d and Hedges' g are standardised differences between two group means (about 0.2 small, 0.5 medium, 0.8 large by convention). r is a correlation.

---

#### 1. Colour

##### 1.1 Colour-emotion associations across nations

**Jonauskaite, Abu-Akel, Dael, Oberfeld, et al. and Mohr (2020).** "Universal patterns in color-emotion associations are further shaped by linguistic and geographic proximity." *Psychological Science* 31(10), 1245-1260. https://doi.org/10.1177/0956797620948810. (Note on the date: the 30-nation paper is 2020. A 2019 paper by the same group is a different study, listed next. A conference or preprint version of the 30-nation work circulated in 2019.)
- Participants and method: 4,598 participants from 30 nations, 22 native languages, aged 15 to 87 (mean 35.4, SD 14.5; 1,114 men), 69 to 490 per nation, online survey. Each rated associations between 12 colour terms and 20 emotion concepts, choosing emotions and an intensity for each colour term. (read: https://api.unil.ch/iris/server/api/core/bitstreams/7d6867e0-d9bc-4341-885c-19643911db2d/content, the peer-reviewed manuscript)
- Result: the average similarity of each nation's colour-emotion pattern to the global pattern was r = .88. Nation-to-global similarity ran from r = .684 (Egypt) to r = .941 (Spain). Associations with a probability of at least 0.4 in the global pattern were: black with sadness, fear and hate; red with love and anger; pink with love, joy and pleasure; grey with sadness and disappointment; yellow with joy; orange with joy and amusement; white with relief. A machine-learning classifier predicted the nation above chance, so local differences exist; nations that share a border or a language were more similar. Examples of local difference reported: sadness went with black everywhere but also with purple for Greeks and with white for the Chinese sample. (read: same URL)
- Limits: colour *terms* were rated, not colour patches, and the task was an association task, not a measure of felt emotion. The survey link was shared online through university communications, e-mail, social media and personal contact, mainly via the co-authors in each country, so the sample is a volunteer one. (read: same URL)

**Jonauskaite, Wicker, Mohr, Dael, Havelka, Papadatou-Pastou, Zhang, Oberfeld (2019).** "A machine learning approach to quantify the specificity of colour-emotion associations and their cultural differences." *Royal Society Open Science* 6, 190741. https://doi.org/10.1098/rsos.190741
- 711 online participants from China, Germany, Greece and the UK associated 12 colour terms with 20 emotion terms in their own languages. A statistical classifier decoded the colour term from the 20 emotion ratings, and the country from the 240 associations, both above chance. The in-group advantage in colour decoding was small, 6.1%. The authors read this as "both universal and culture-specific". (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/31598303)

**Jonauskaite and Mohr (2025).** "Do we feel colours? A systematic review of 128 years of psychological research linking colours and emotions." *Psychonomic Bulletin & Review* 32, 1457-1486. https://doi.org/10.3758/s13423-024-02615-z
- Systematic review of 132 peer-reviewed articles, 1895 to 2022, 42,266 participants from 64 countries. Reported pattern: light colours go with positive emotions and dark colours with negative; red with empowering and high-arousal emotions, both positive and negative; yellow and orange with positive, high-arousal emotions; blue, green, green-blue and white with positive, low-arousal emotions; pink with positive emotions; purple with empowering emotions; grey with negative, low-arousal emotions; black with negative, high-arousal emotions. Effects are driven by lightness, saturation and hue. Most correspondences are many-to-many. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/39806242)
- Limits stated by the authors: most correspondences were tested as abstract associations, so it "remains to be seen" whether they translate to experienced emotion in specific contexts. (read: same abstract)

**Adams and Osgood (1973).** "A cross-cultural study of the affective meanings of color." *Journal of Cross-Cultural Psychology* 4(2), 135-156. https://doi.org/10.1177/002202217300400201
- I could not open the paper (the publisher page returned 403). Search summaries describe semantic-differential ratings of colour concepts in about 20 to 23 cultures, with red rated strong and active and broad cross-cultural similarity; sources disagree on the number of cultures (20 or 23). (summary only: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/397504799_Colour_and_Mood_Universally_vs_Culturally_Constructed_Emotional_Responses and https://arxiv.org/pdf/1505.06532). Treat the details as unconfirmed.

##### 1.2 Saturation, brightness, hue, and measured arousal

**Valdez and Mehrabian (1994).** "Effects of color on emotions." *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General* 123(4), 394-409. https://doi.org/10.1037/0096-3445.123.4.394
- Method: emotional reactions to hue, saturation and brightness of Munsell colour chips, on the Pleasure-Arousal-Dominance model. (Participant numbers are not in the abstract.)
- Result: regression equations on standardised variables: Pleasure = .69 Brightness + .22 Saturation; Arousal = -.31 Brightness + .60 Saturation; Dominance = -.76 Brightness + .32 Saturation. Brightness effects were nearly the same for chromatic and achromatic colours. Most pleasant hues: blue, blue-green, green, red-purple, purple, purple-blue; least pleasant: yellow and green-yellow. Most arousing: green-yellow, blue-green, green; least arousing: purple-blue and yellow-red. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/7996122)
- Limits: not in the abstract. The brightness result is the opposite sign for arousal and dominance (brighter colours rated less arousing and less dominant), a point worth setting beside Wilms and Oberfeld below, who found brighter colours more arousing.

**Wilms and Oberfeld (2018).** "Color and emotion: effects of hue, saturation, and brightness." *Psychological Research* 82, 896-914. https://doi.org/10.1007/s00426-017-0880-8
- Method: N = 62, each viewed 27 chromatic colours (3 hues, blue, green, red, crossed with 3 saturations and 3 brightnesses) plus 3 brightness-matched greys on an LED display for 30 s, then rated valence and arousal; skin conductance and heart rate were recorded.
- Result: saturated and brighter colours were rated higher in arousal; arousal rose from blue and green to red. Valence was highest for saturated and bright colours, and depended on hue (blue rated higher than the other hues only when saturation was high). Saturated and bright colours produced stronger skin-conductance responses. Achromatic colours produced a short heart-rate deceleration; chromatic colours an acceleration. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/28612080)
- Disagreement with Valdez and Mehrabian on brightness and arousal: Valdez and Mehrabian's equation has a negative brightness weight for arousal (-.31); Wilms and Oberfeld report higher arousal for brighter colours. Differences in method (rating of colour chips as stimuli, versus a lit display viewed for 30 s with physiological recording) are visible from the two abstracts; I have not read either full paper to see how the authors account for it.

**Kaya and Epps (2004).** "Relationship between color and emotion: a study of college students." *College Student Journal* 38(3), 396-405. ERIC EJ706686. https://eric.ed.gov/?id=EJ706686
- Method: 98 college students at a public university in the south-eastern United States gave emotional responses to 13 Munsell colours (5 principal hues, 5 intermediate hues, 3 achromatic) and their reasons.
- Result: principal hues produced the most positive responses, then intermediate hues, then achromatic colours. Green gave mostly positive feelings (relaxation, comfort), tied by respondents to nature. Green-yellow gave the fewest positive responses (sickness, disgust). Among achromatics, white was most positive, then black, then grey. (read: https://eric.ed.gov/?id=EJ706686.) A search summary adds that grey was linked to negative emotions in 89.8% of responses (summary only: https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/Relationship-between-color-and-emotion:-A-study-of-Kaya-Epps/e227372d80f0d1853894d5eb452c74c8f78a95c2).
- Limits: one convenience student sample from one region; free-response reasons rather than a pre-registered design. Not stated on the page: effect sizes.

##### 1.3 Why a colour is liked: ecological valence theory

**Palmer and Schloss (2010).** "An ecological valence theory of human color preference." *PNAS* 107(19), 8877-8882. https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.0906172107
- Claim: average colour preference follows people's average affective response to objects strongly associated with that colour (blues with clear sky and clean water liked; browns with faeces and rotten food disliked). The theory fits better than a cone-contrast account and a colorimetric account, with fewer free parameters. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/20421475)
- Numbers: the 2010 abstract gives none. Taylor and Franklin (below) report that Palmer and Schloss found the weighted affective valence estimate (WAVE) explained 80% of the variation in preference across colours. (read: https://europepmc.org/article/MED/22302645.) An earlier conference version by the same two authors (Schloss and Palmer, Cognitive Science Society proceedings, 2009) reports: 48 participants rated preference for the 37 colours of the Berkeley Color Project; WAVE came from 74 participants who described objects and 98 who rated them; r = .82 between WAVE and mean preference, 67% of variance with a single predictor, versus 36% for a cone-contrast model and 57% for a colorimetric model, each with three predictors. (read: https://escholarship.org/content/qt9fr2s1z1/qt9fr2s1z1_noSplash_852a660b6f43f17c2f472fba69568a3c.pdf). The 80% and the 67% are different analyses in different versions; I have not opened the PNAS full text to see which figure it states.

**Taylor and Franklin (2012).** "The relationship between color-object associations and color preference: further investigation of ecological valence theory." *Psychonomic Bulletin & Review* 19, 190-197. https://doi.org/10.3758/s13423-012-0222-1
- Replication and extension. WAVE and preference shared 74% of variance for males but 45% for females. The number of objects associated with a colour correlated inversely with its preference, and for the whole sample and for women this relation was not significantly weaker than WAVE and preference. The authors conclude the WAVE's success was partly due to this object-count relation, identify "constraints" on the theory, and still give "general support" for the link. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/22302645)

**Taylor, Schloss, Palmer and Franklin (2013).** "Color preferences in infants and adults are different." *Psychonomic Bulletin & Review* 20, 916-922. https://doi.org/10.3758/s13423-013-0411-6
- Looking-time task, all pairs of 8 colours (4 hues at 2 lightnesses). Adults looked longer at colours they liked. Infants showed a hue-by-lightness interaction different from the adult pattern (stronger preference for dark yellow, weaker for light blue). (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/23435629)

**Strauss, Schloss and Palmer (2013).** "Color preferences change after experience with liked/disliked colored objects." *Psychonomic Bulletin & Review* 20, 935-943. https://doi.org/10.3758/s13423-013-0423-2
- Exposure to liked or disliked red and green objects shifted preference for red and green patches, but only when participants evaluated the objects as they saw them; the change decreased substantially over 24 hours. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/23616112)

**Hurlbert and Ling (2007).** "Biological components of sex differences in color preference." *Current Biology* 17, R623-R625. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cub.2007.06.022
- The abstract is not available at Europe PMC. Described in the Schloss and Palmer proceedings paper above as an account based on cone contrasts (L-M and S-(L+M)) plus sex differences. The account explained 70% of variance in Hurlbert and Ling's own data and 19% in Palmer and Schloss's data. (read: the proceedings text above.)

##### 1.4 Red and achievement, and colour-in-context theory

**Elliot and Maier (2012).** "Color-in-context theory." *Advances in Experimental Social Psychology* 45, 61-125. https://doi.org/10.1016/B978-0-12-394286-9.00002-0, and **Elliot and Maier (2014)** "Color psychology: effects of perceiving color on psychological functioning in humans." *Annual Review of Psychology* 65, 95-120. https://doi.org/10.1146/annurev-psych-010213-115035
- I did not open either. Elliot (2015, below) summarises the theory: it "draws on social learning, as well as biology"; some colour responses are presumed to come from repeated pairing of a colour with concepts and experiences, others from a biologically engrained predisposition reinforced by learning; the meaning of a colour depends on context. (read: the summary in https://europepmc.org/article/PMC4383146 full text.)

**Elliot, Maier, Moller, Friedman and Meinhardt (2007).** "Color and psychological functioning: the effect of red on performance attainment." *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General* 136(1), 154-168. https://doi.org/10.1037/0096-3445.136.1.154
- Six experiments: brief perception of red before an important test (e.g. an IQ test) impaired performance, outside awareness; red also linked to avoidance motivation (task choice, cortical activation). (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/17324089)

**Elliot, Kayser, Greitemeyer, Lichtenfeld, Gramzow, Maier and Liu (2010).** "Red, rank, and romance in women viewing men." *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General* 139, 399-417. https://doi.org/10.1037/a0019689
- Seven experiments: women rated men as more attractive and sexually desirable on a red background or in red clothing; perceived status mediated it. No effect on men's perceptions of other men. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/20677892)

**Hill and Barton (2005).** "Red enhances human performance in contests." *Nature* 435, 293. https://doi.org/10.1038/435293a
- Across several sports, wearing red was associated with a higher probability of winning. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/15902246)

**Failed replications and reviews of the red effects**
- **Gnambs (2020).** "Limited evidence for the effect of red color on cognitive performance: a meta-analysis." *Psychonomic Bulletin & Review* 27, 1374-1382. https://doi.org/10.3758/s13423-020-01772-1. 67 effect sizes from 38 samples. Anagram and knowledge tests: d = -0.06 and -0.04 (no significant difference). Reasoning tests: pooled d = -0.34, 95% CI [-0.61, -0.06] (lower scores under red). Effects were substantially larger in initial studies than later ones; after correcting for publication bias, "no evidential value" for an effect of red on intellectual performance. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/32696125)
- **Gnambs, Kovacs and Stiglbauer (2019/2020).** "Processing the word red and intellectual performance: four replication attempts." *Collabra: Psychology* 6(1), 3 (preprint https://doi.org/10.31234/osf.io/4ertj). Experiments 1 and 2 (N = 69 and 104), direct replications of Lichtenfeld, Maier, Elliot and Pekrun (2009): d = 0.04 and -0.23 on verbal reasoning. Experiments 3 and 4 (N = 103 and 1,149): d = 0.19 and 0.01 on general knowledge. (read: preprint abstract, DOI https://doi.org/10.31234/osf.io/4ertj, via Europe PMC)
- **Elliot (2015).** "Color and psychological functioning: a review of theoretical and empirical work." *Frontiers in Psychology* 6, 368. https://doi.org/10.3389/fpsyg.2015.00368. The theory's own proponent concludes the literature is "at a nascent stage", that much research remains underpowered, that small-sample first demonstrations tend to overestimate effects, and recommends "patience and prudence regarding conclusions about theory, findings, and real-world application". (read: abstract and full text, https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC4383146)
- **Red in combat sports.** Hill and Barton's claim is disputed; a 2024 meta-analysis in *Scientific Reports* reports 6,589 contest outcomes in boxing, taekwondo and wrestling (Olympic Games 1996-2020, World Boxing Championships 2005-2021) with red winning 50.5% overall (not significantly different from chance); close contests before 2005 showed 56.8% red wins. (summary only: https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-024-81373-3.) A search summary also notes Hagemann et al. (2008) found referees in taekwondo gave more points to competitors in red (summary only; I did not open it).

##### 1.5 Colour terms

**Berlin and Kay (1969).** *Basic Color Terms: Their Universality and Evolution.* University of California Press. (memory for the citation.) A search summary gives the claim: languages with few basic colour terms follow a fixed order, black and white first, then red, then green and yellow, then blue, then brown, then purple, pink, orange and grey; the "evolutionary stages" reading is the most controversial part, and the World Color Survey (from 1976) was set up to test it. (summary only: https://www.icsi.berkeley.edu/icsi/gazette/2010/03/world-color-survey, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_Color_Terms.)
- **Kay and Regier (2003).** "Resolving the question of color naming universals." *PNAS* 100, 9085-9089. https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1532837100. Tests on colour-naming data from industrialised and nonindustrialised societies "show that strong universal tendencies in color naming exist across both sorts of language". (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/12855768)

##### 1.6 Colour and music (cross-modal, mediated by emotion)

**Whiteford, Schloss, Helwig and Palmer (2018).** "Color, music, and emotion: Bach to the Blues." *i-Perception* 9(6). https://doi.org/10.1177/2041669518808535. Matches of music to colour were mediated by emotion ratings; loud, punchy, distorted music went with darker, redder, more saturated colours; partialling out emotional content removed the lower-level correlations. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/30479734)

---

#### 2. Shape

##### 2.1 Curved and angular contours

**Bar and Neta (2006).** "Humans prefer curved visual objects." *Psychological Science* 17(8), 645-648. https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1467-9280.2006.01759.x
- Hypothesis: sharp contours convey threat and trigger a negative bias. Participants judged novel objects, patterns and everyday objects for liking; curved stimuli were liked more than sharp ones. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/16913943.) Participants and timing: 14 participants, 84 ms exposures, like/dislike choice (read: Gomez-Puerto, Munar and Nadal 2016 review, https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC4709714).
- A neuroimaging follow-up (Bar and Neta 2007) reported greater amygdala activation for sharp stimuli (read: same review; sample size not given there).
- Limits (from the review above): small samples; mostly Western samples; some "sharp" stimuli were only pointed; Bar and Neta (2008) later recast the finding as possibly developmental learning; Leder et al. (2011) found the curved preference replicated for the original stimuli but, with 20 real objects in round and sharp versions, only for positive or neutral objects, which they took to show that meaning can override curvature; Vartanian et al. (2013) found that sharp-angled rooms did not raise amygdala activity.

**Silvia and Barona (2009).** "Do people prefer curved objects? Angularity, expertise, and aesthetic preference." *Empirical Studies of the Arts* 27(1), 25-42. (memory for the citation.) The review above reports: Experiment 1 (hexagons and circles in varying numbers) only non-experts preferred circles, experts slightly preferred hexagons; Experiment 2 (abstract curved and angular shapes) only experts preferred the curved ones. (read, second hand: https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC4709714; sample sizes not given.)

**Palumbo, Ruta and Bertamini (2015).** "Comparing angular and curved shapes in terms of implicit associations and approach/avoidance responses." *PLoS ONE* 10(10), e0140043. https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0140043
- Implicit Association Test: curved polygons associated with safe, positive concepts and female names; angular polygons with danger, negative concepts and male names. A manikin approach/avoid task: response times to approach or avoid angular polygons did not differ, but participants were faster and more accurate moving the manikin toward curved shapes. The authors conclude that preference for curvature "cannot derive entirely from an association of angles with threat". (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/26460610)

**Chuquichambi, Vartanian, Skov, Corradi, Nadal, Silvia and Munar (2022).** "How universal is preference for visual curvature? A systematic review and meta-analysis." *Annals of the New York Academy of Sciences* 1518, 151-165. https://doi.org/10.1111/nyas.14919
- 61 studies, 106 independent samples, 309 effect sizes; three-level random-effects Hedges' g = 0.39 (medium). Moderated by presentation time, stimulus type, expertise and task. Conclusion: the preference is "reliable but not universal" and influenced by factors other than perceptual information. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/36285721)

**Gomez-Puerto, Munar and Nadal (2016).** "Preference for curvature: a historical and conceptual framework." *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience* 9, 712. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4709714/ (read: full text via Europe PMC, https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC4709714). The review reports that terminology varies (curved/straight, round/sharp, curved/angular), that evidence on a threat account is mixed, and that cross-cultural data are scarce (a Ghana conference abstract is cited without numbers).

##### 2.2 Angularity and threat in faces and simple forms

**Aronoff, Barclay and Stevenson (1988).** "The recognition of threatening facial stimuli." *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology* 54(4), 647-655. https://doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.54.4.647
- Two studies. Masks from non-Western cultures with threatening displays contained more of the facial characteristics that distinguished threatening from non-threatening drawings by two American samples. Responses to sets of diagonal, angular and curvilinear stimuli suggested that angularity and diagonality can evoke the subjective responses that convey threat. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/3367283)
- Aronoff, Woike and Hyman (1992), "Which are the stimuli in facial displays of anger and happiness? Configurational bases of emotion recognition," *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology* 62, 1050-1066: I did not open it; it is cited as finding curved lines more pleasant than straight or angled ones (read, second hand: Hu, Lyu and Liu, see 3.2).

**Larson, Aronoff and Stearns (2007).** "The shape of threat: simple geometric forms evoke rapid and sustained capture of attention." *Emotion* 7(3), 526-534. https://doi.org/10.1037/1528-3542.7.3.526
- Five visual-search experiments: a downward-pointing V among other shapes was detected faster, and in some cases more accurately, than identical upward-pointing shapes; homogeneous fields of downward V's sometimes slowed responses. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/17683209; number of participants not in abstract)

**Larson, Aronoff, Sarinopoulos and Zhu (2009).** "Recognizing threat: a simple geometric shape activates neural circuitry for threat detection." *Journal of Cognitive Neuroscience* 21(8), 1523-1535. https://doi.org/10.1162/jocn.2009.21111
- fMRI: downward-pointing triangles produced greater activation than identical upward-pointing ones in the amygdala, subgenual anterior cingulate, superior temporal gyrus, fusiform gyrus and extrastriate regions. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/18823242)

**Larson, Aronoff and Steuer (2012).** "Simple geometric shapes are implicitly associated with affective value." *Motivation and Emotion* 36, 404-413. https://doi.org/10.1007/s11031-011-9249-2
- Two Implicit Association Test studies with downward and upward triangles and circles: participants were faster to categorise downward-pointing triangles as unpleasant than as neutral or pleasant; the effect was specific to downward shapes with an acute angle. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/30122793)

**Salgado-Montejo, Salgado, Alvarado and Spence (accepted manuscript, 2015).** "Simple lines and shapes are associated with, and communicate, distinct emotions." Oxford University Research Archive. (read: https://ora.ox.ac.uk/objects/uuid:be8992e0-cb8c-4ad7-bba9-8390de34a515/files/m9bdfe96ff037ef4ea6f9c9913ac8c6ab.) Experiment 1 (54 participants from the UK and Colombia): easiest to pair a concave line (a "smile") with happy and a convex line with sad. Experiments 2 and 3 show eyes or eye-like dots change the ratings. I did not confirm the final published version of this paper.

**Walker (2012).** "Cross-sensory correspondences and cross talk between dimensions of connotative meaning: visual angularity is hard, high-pitched, and bright." *Attention, Perception, & Psychophysics* 74, 1792-1809. https://doi.org/10.3758/s13414-012-0341-9
- Speeded classification: congruity effects between shape angularity and each of hardness, pitch and brightness; angular shapes pattern with hard, high-pitched and bright. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/22833281)

##### 2.3 Kiki and bouba

- **Köhler (1929, 1947), Ramachandran and Hubbard (2001).** Köhler's *Gestalt Psychology* (1929; 2nd ed. 1947) first reported the nonsense-word match (maluma/takete, later bouba/kiki); Ramachandran and Hubbard, "Synaesthesia: a window into perception, thought and language," *Journal of Consciousness Studies* 8(12), 3-34, 2001, popularised it. (memory for both citations; I did not find a page to open. The Maurer et al. and Styles and Gawne abstracts below confirm the lineage: "Köhler, 1947; Ramachandran and Hubbard, 2001", and "Eighty-seven years ago, Köhler reported that the majority of students picked the same answer".) A figure of about 95% agreement is quoted for Ramachandran and Hubbard (memory; unverified).
- **Maurer, Pathman and Mondloch (2006).** "The shape of boubas: sound-shape correspondences in toddlers and adults." *Developmental Science* 9, 316-322. https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1467-7687.2006.00495.x. 2.5-year-olds and adults (n = 20 per age), four pairs of rounded and pointed shapes and four pairs of nonsense words differing in vowel sound; both ages matched rounded vowels to rounder shapes. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/16669803)
- **Bremner, Caparos, Davidoff, de Fockert, Linnell and Spence (2013).** "'Bouba' and 'Kiki' in Namibia?" *Cognition* 126, 165-172. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cognition.2012.09.007. The effect was observed in the Himba of northern Namibia, a remote population without a written language, but the Himba did not map carbonation to angular shapes and tended to match less bitter chocolate to angular shapes, the opposite of Westerners. The authors conclude cultural-environmental factors help shape crossmodal correspondences. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/23121711)
- **Ćwiek et al. (2022).** "The bouba/kiki effect is robust across cultures and writing systems." *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B* 377, 20200390. https://doi.org/10.1098/rstb.2020.0390. Online experiment, 976 participants collected, 917 retained, 25 languages, nine language families, 10 writing systems. Estimated congruent proportion 72% (95% credible interval 56% to 82%); speakers of languages with Roman script only marginally more likely to show the effect; non-Roman-script speakers on average 63%. (read: abstract and full text, https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC8591387)
- **Failure and limit: Styles and Gawne (2017).** "When does maluma/takete fail? Two key failures and a meta-analysis suggest that phonology and phonotactics matter." *i-Perception* 8(4). https://doi.org/10.1177/2041669517724807. Speakers of Syuba (Nepal) showed no preference matching "kiki" and "bubu" to spiky and curvy shapes; a meta-analysis suggested failures occur when test words do not follow the sound structure of the language. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/28890777)
- **Fort and Schwartz (2022).** *Scientific Reports* 12, 19172. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-022-23623-w. Argues the effect rests on two acoustic cues (spectral balance and temporal continuity) tied to the physics of round and spiky objects. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/36357511)
- Autism: Prescott et al. (2026) and Król et al. (2026) report a weaker bouba/kiki effect in autistic participants (read: abstracts, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/42578081, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/42012231); I include them only as a stated limit on universality.

##### 2.4 Feeling value of lines

**Lundholm (1921).** "The affective tone of lines: experimental researches." *Psychological Review* 28, 43-60. (read, second hand.) Eight participants drew lines to match feeling words; sharp lines went with feelings such as agitating, hard, furious; curved lines with gentle and quiet, and also sad and lazy (read: https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC4709714). Another summary: 48 adjectives grouped in 13 sets, lines for synonyms of one set were similar across participants; lines dominated by sharp angles went with unpleasant, intense emotions, rounded ones with happy, calm emotions (read: Hu, Lyu and Liu, see 3.2). I did not read Lundholm's paper.

**Poffenberger and Barrows (1924).** "The feeling value of lines." *Journal of Applied Psychology* 8, 187-205. (read, second hand.) 500 adults matched Lundholm's participants' lines to a list of feelings; the results "confirmed" Lundholm (read: Gomez-Puerto et al., same review). A search summary says they also found that angular lines seemed sadder, contradicting Lundholm (summary only: a search result that quoted the review; I did not find that sentence in the review text and so do not rely on it).

**Hevner (1935).** "Experimental studies of the affective value of colors and lines." *Journal of Applied Psychology* 19, 385-398. I did not open it. The Gomez-Puerto review says Hevner "confirmed" Lundholm using a more complex set of stimuli including lines and abstract shapes (read: same review). A search summary describes undergraduate groups of 40 to 45, line designs (circles, squares, angles, waves) in blue or red, adjective checklists (summary only: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/232438050_Experimental_studies_of_the_affective_value_of_colors_and_lines).

**Collier (1996).** "Affective synesthesia: extracting emotion space from simple perceptual stimuli." *Motivation and Emotion* 20(1), 1-32. https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02251005. I could not find an abstract or text; see "What I could not find".

---

#### 3. Line weight and line quality

I found one direct experimental study of drawn line thickness against affective adjectives, and a body of typeface-weight studies. The idea that thick lines feel strong and thin lines fragile is stated widely in art instruction; I found no experiment in a psychology database aimed at that claim in drawings (see the end).

##### 3.1 Thickness of line segments, semantic differential

**Albertazzi, Canal, Micciolo and Hachen (2021).** "The perceptual organisation of visual elements: lines." *Brain Sciences* 11(12), 1585. https://doi.org/10.3390/brainsci11121585
- Method: line segments varying in thickness (average 0.5, 1.25, 3.1, 7.8 and 19.5 mm), type (straight or curved), orientation and colour. Two experiments (30 and 26 participants, University of Trento students) judged whether a segment was a line or a surface; Experiments 3 and 4 (28 and 25 participants) rated the segments on 15 pairs of opposite adjectives (an Osgood semantic differential).
- Result: thickness and type were the variables with the greatest influence; colour less; orientation generally not significant. For thickness, the highest odds ratios were for heavy/lightweight and strong/weak. Much lower values were found for cold/warm (thicker lines warmer), feminine/masculine (thinner lines feminine, thicker masculine), blunt/sharp (thinner lines sharp, thicker blunt) and bound/free (thinner free, thicker bound). For type: straight lines were rated geometrical, flat, hard, static, rough, sharp, bound, sour, frigid, masculine, cold and passive; curved lines organic, rounded, soft, dynamic, fluffy, blunt, free, sweet, sensual, feminine, warm and active. (read: abstract and full text, https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC8699471. I did not extract the odds-ratio values; they are in the paper's Table 3.)
- Limits: Italian university students, short experiments, 25 to 30 participants each, adjectives translated from Italian; for some pairs the authors say the pairs "do not seem to characterise the variable thickness" even though choice was not random.

##### 3.2 Lines and implied motion

**Hu, Lyu and Liu.** "Is the emotional mapping of lines caused by the motion they imply?" Proceedings of the Annual Meeting of the Cognitive Science Society (year not stated in the text I extracted). https://escholarship.org/content/qt3gp9g012/qt3gp9g012_noSplash_80573abf8f3e99f51d0e3784aff2d80f.pdf (read.) 87 line patterns, static and as a dot moving along the line at slow and fast speed; participants chose descriptors from 29 emotion words. Implied motion shifted the ratings toward positive and high arousal compared with the static line, and speed affected arousal. The authors say causality "needs further study". The paper also summarises earlier work: curved lines described as pleasant, soft, feminine, quiet; straight lines as direct, aggressive, bland, honest, and that people "show a preference for curves, [but] there is no aversion to angles" (Bertamini, Palumbo, Gheorghes and Galatsidas) (read, second hand).

##### 3.3 Typeface weight (stroke thickness in letters)

- **Atilgan, Palmen, Kucuk and Sawyer (2025 poster).** "The impact of font weight on expressiveness beyond Latin: insights from Arabic and English." Vision Sciences Society poster, The Readability Consortium. 50 bilingual (English/Arabic) participants, 18 to 49, read sentences in Noto Sans at varying weights and chose adjectives from a list of 21. Font weight significantly influenced adjective choice (p < .001); lighter weights were associated with "calm" and bolder weights with "active" in both scripts (both p < .001); bold Arabic was rated more "childlike" than bold English. (read: https://thereadabilityconsortium.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/08/VSS25_Poster_Arabic-1.pdf.) Limits: poster, not peer-reviewed as far as I can tell; one typeface.
- **Shaikh, Chaparro and Fox (2006).** "Perception of fonts: perceived personality traits and uses." *Usability News* 8(1). Online survey; participants rated 20 on-screen fonts on 15 adjective pairs and chose appropriate uses. Personality was attributed by design family (serif, sans-serif, modern, monospace, script/funny), not stroke weight in isolation. The introduction notes the popular description of "masculine" fonts as blocky and bold and "feminine" as fine and sleek, quoting a BBC programme. (read: https://soma.sbcc.edu/users/russotti/113/personality_Shaikh.pdf; participants' number not stated in the part I read.)
- **Typeface "persona" studies (summary only).** Poffenberger and Franken (1923), "A study of the appropriateness of type faces," *Journal of Applied Psychology* 7(4), 312-329; Brumberger (2003), "The rhetoric of typography," *Technical Communication* 50(2), 206-231; Henderson, Giese and Cote (2004), *Journal of Marketing* 68, 60-72. I read none of them. (summary only: https://www.academia.edu/8402713/The_Rhetoric_of_Typography_The_Persona_of_Typeface_and_Text.) A search summary also reports that increased font weight enhanced ruggedness and competence and decreased weight signalled sincerity in a brand-personality study, with the weight effect appearing in only one of three studies (summary only: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/256016577_Using_Type_Font_Characteristics_to_Communicate_Brand_Personality_of_New_Brands).
- **Perceived thickness itself.** A thickness illusion: horizontal lines are perceived as thicker than vertical lines of equal width (*Vision* 3(1), 2019, https://doi.org/10.3390/vision3010001; summary only: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6802759/). Relevant when comparing line weights of different orientation.
- **Japanese typefaces.** arXiv 2309.06743 (Chujo, Suzuki, Hautasaari 2023) links typeface design to impressions and advice acceptance. A search summary said fonts conveying both fear and sadness tended to have extremely thin lines; the arXiv abstract page I opened does not say that, so that claim is unconfirmed. (read: abstract only, https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.06743)

---

#### 4. Size and proportion

##### 4.1 Size, power and dominance

**Thomsen, Frankenhuis, Ingold-Smith and Carey (2011).** "Big and mighty: preverbal infants mentally represent social dominance." *Science* 331, 477-480. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1199198
- Five studies, 144 infants. 10- to 13-month-olds, but not 8-month-olds, recognised conflicting goals between two novel agents and used the agents' relative size to predict the outcome of the first dominance contest. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/21273490)

**Duguid and Goncalo (2012).** "Living large: the powerful overestimate their own height." *Psychological Science* 23(1), 36-40. https://doi.org/10.1177/0956797611422915
- Three experiments: high power, relative to low, went with smaller estimates of a pole's height relative to self (Exp. 1), larger estimates of one's own height (Exp. 2), and choice of a taller avatar (Exp. 3). (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/22173738)

**Giessner and Schubert (2007)** found that a longer vertical line in an organisation chart raised judged power (d = 0.55 in the original Study 1a). **Failed replication:** the Many Labs 2 project found d = 0.03, 95% CI [-0.01, 0.08] in a high-powered replication. The authors' own 2019 pre-registered report confirmed Study 3a (a leader described as powerful has his box placed higher, d = 0.86 originally) and confirmed that rank order of vertical position, but not the magnitude of elevation, affects power judgments. (read: https://online.ucpress.edu/collabra/article/5/1/25/113011/Power-and-Vertical-Positions-in-an-Organization, text extracted from the PDF at https://pdfs.semanticscholar.org/6f11/becd30588c01864ffa8eb5e0a44c9d980c2b.pdf). The same report cites older work that taller people reach higher positions and are seen as stronger and more dominant (Judge and Cable 2004; Schwartz, Tesser and Powell 1982), which I did not open.

**Klein et al. (2018).** "Many Labs 2: investigating variation in replicability across sample and setting." *Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science* 1, 443-490. 28 findings, 125 samples, 15,305 participants, 36 countries; 54% replicated at p < .05; median d was 0.60 in the originals and 0.15 in the replications. (read: abstract via Europe PMC, DOI as above)

**Sundar and Noseworthy (2014).** "Place the logo high or low? Using conceptual metaphors of power in packaging design." *Journal of Marketing* 78(5), 138-151. https://doi.org/10.1509/jm.13.0253. A search summary says powerful brands were preferred more with the logo high on the package and less powerful brands with the logo low. (summary only: https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=2710347)

I found no study that varied the *size* of a printed or drawn symbol and measured felt dominance or importance directly; the size results above concern body or agent size and height.

##### 4.2 Proportion: the golden section and Fechner's rectangles

**Fechner (1876).** *Vorschule der Aesthetik.* Leipzig: Breitkopf und Hartel. (memory for the citation.) As described by Green (1995): ten rectangles with side ratios from 1:1 to 2.5:1; subjects chose the most pleasing and many also the least pleasing; 347 responses (the number of subjects is not clear from Fechner's report); 35.0% chose the golden rectangle (ratio 1.62:1), 20.6% the 1.5:1 and 20.0% the 1.77:1; the least favoured were 2.5:1 (40.9% of "least pleasing" choices) and the square (31.9%); none chose the golden rectangle as least pleasing. Of nine ellipses Fechner found 42% preferred the 1.5:1 ellipse and 16.7% the "golden" ellipse. Green notes that the golden rectangle was seventh of ten in order of elongation, not at the centre, and that many secondary accounts misreport the method and results (e.g. "over 75% chose one rectangle"). (read: https://www.yorku.ca/christo/papers/Green.golden.Perception-1995.pdf)

**Lalo (1908).** Replicated Fechner's procedure with simultaneous presentation; 30.3% chose the golden rectangle (Fechner 35.0%); more chose the extremes (11.7% the square, 15.3% the 2.5:1 rectangle) than in Fechner's data; none chose the golden as worst. (read, second hand: Green 1995, same URL)

**Green (1995).** "All that glitters: a review of psychological research on the aesthetics of the golden section." *Perception* 24, 937-968. https://www.yorku.ca/christo/papers/Green.golden.Perception-1995.pdf. Review from the 1860s to 1995. Conclusion: "there seems to be, in fact, real psychological effects associated with the golden section, but that they are relatively sensitive to careless methodological practices." (read: abstract)

**Disputing and failing results, side by side**
- **Piehl (1976).** "The golden section: an artifact of stimulus range and demand characteristics." *Perceptual and Motor Skills* 43, 47-50. https://doi.org/10.2466/pms.1976.43.1.47. 90 subjects, rectangles presented in three ranges with the golden section at different positions: the rectangles at both extremes of each range were chosen first most often; the golden section was chosen most only under a condition that reinforced it. Conclusion: an artifact of stimulus range and demand characteristics. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/958835)
- **Russell (2000).** "Testing the aesthetic significance of the golden-section rectangle." *Perception* 29, 1413-1422. https://doi.org/10.1068/p3037. Study 1: mean side ratios of paintings in five subject categories gave no evidence for the golden section (the ratios varied with the subject matter, e.g. portrait versus landscape). Study 2: when participants produced the most pleasing four-sided shape, the means differed significantly from the golden section for "portrait painting" and "landscape painting" instructions but approximated it for "context free" horizontal and vertical rectangles. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/11257965)
- **Phillips, Norman and Beers (2010).** "Fechner's aesthetics revisited." *Seeing and Perceiving* 23, 263-271. https://doi.org/10.1163/187847510X516412. States that "there has been a terribly mixed bag of results" in replications, that recent findings suggest Fechner's results were "anomalous and/or due primarily to methodology" (Green 1995; Höge 1997), and finds in their own experiment with more naturalistic stimuli that subjects prefer very simple or very complex objects, contrary to the golden-section hypothesis. (read: https://www.wku.edu/psychological-sciences/labs/vision_and_haptics/downloads/phillips_norman_aesthetics2010.pdf)
- **Höge (1997).** "The golden section hypothesis: its last funeral." *Empirical Studies of the Arts* 15, 233-255. https://doi.org/10.2190/2PNH-8TT0-EMC5-FTW5. I did not open it; Phillips et al. cite it as an extensive review that found mixed results. (read, second hand.)
- **Stieger and Swami (2015).** "Time to let go? No automatic aesthetic preference for the golden ratio in art pictures." *Psychology of Aesthetics, Creativity, and the Arts* 9(1), 91-100. Implicit Association Test with art images in which the main object sat at the golden ratio versus the centre (Studies 1 and 2) or a 3/4 position (Study 3): no clear preference for the golden ratio, explicit or implicit; the authors suggest any preference may depend on art expertise. (read: abstract, https://kris.kl.ac.at/en/publications/time-to-let-go-no-automatic-aesthetic-preference-for-the-golden-r. A search snippet said the golden ratio was preferred only over the 3/4 proportion in a different reading; the abstract page I read does not say this, so unconfirmed.)
- **Naini (2024).** "The golden ratio: dispelling the myth." *Maxillofacial Plastic and Reconstructive Surgery* 46, 2. https://doi.org/10.1186/s40902-024-00411-2. Reviews clinical claims that the golden ratio marks beautiful faces and idealised proportions and finds no convincing evidence. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/38228978)

---

#### 5. Texture and lustre

**Etzi, Spence, Zampini and Gallace (2016).** "When sandpaper is 'Kiki' and satin is 'Bouba': an exploration of the associations between words, emotional states, and the tactile attributes of everyday materials." *Multisensory Research* 29, 133-155. https://doi.org/10.1163/22134808-00002497
- Cotton, satin, tinfoil, sandpaper and an abrasive sponge stroked along the forearm at 5 cm/s; participants rated them on non-word pairs (Kiki/Bouba etc.) and adjectives. Smoother textures went with round-sounding non-words (maluma) and rougher with sharp-sounding ones (takete); smooth with "bright" and "quiet", rough with "dim" and "loud"; textures were also tied to emotional labels. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/27311294; sample size not in the abstract)

**Ackerman, Nocera and Bargh (2010).** "Incidental haptic sensations influence social judgments and decisions." *Science* 328, 1712-1715. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1189993
- Six experiments: heavy or light clipboards, rough or smooth puzzles, hard or soft objects. Heavy objects made job candidates seem more important; rough objects made a social interaction seem more difficult; hard objects increased rigidity in negotiation. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/20576894)
- Limits: a pre-registered replication I saw named, "Incidental haptic sensations may not influence social judgments: a purely confirmatory replication attempt of Study 1" (*Journal of Articles in Support of the Null Hypothesis* 14(2)), tested the heavy-clipboard result, not the rough-puzzle one (summary only: https://www.jasnh.com/pdf/Vol14-No2-article1.pdf). I found no replication of the rough-puzzle result.

**Bertheaux, Toscano, Fortunier, Roux, Charier and Borg (2019).** "Emotion measurements through the touch of materials surfaces." *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience* 13, 455. https://doi.org/10.3389/fnhum.2019.00455
- 25 university students (13 women), blind touch of 12 materials (polymers, sandpapers, wood, velvet, fur); pupil size varied with emotional intensity, larger for pleasant or unpleasant materials than neutral ones, with differences by gender. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/32009917)

**Silvia, Rodriguez, Cotter and Christensen (2021).** "Aesthetic preference for glossy materials: an attempted replication and extension." *Behavioral Sciences* 11(4), 44. https://doi.org/10.3390/bs11040044
- 100 adults viewed landscape photographs on high-gloss or matte-sprayed identical paper. Glossy pictures were rated more attractive, but d = -0.23, 95% CI [-0.62, 0.16], not significant. The effect was moderated by the aesthetic-appreciation facet of Openness: high appreciation, attractive regardless; low appreciation, strong preference for gloss. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/33810411)
- The earlier work this replicates, on a preference for glossy and shiny materials, I saw only in search summaries: a study of metal objects (silver coins and polished copper cylinders preferred when shiny) and consumer-product studies attributing a preference for gloss to perceived newness (summary only: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/317383269_Do_People_Have_a_Thing_for_Bling_Examining_Aesthetic_Preferences_for_Shiny_Objects; https://www.emerald.com/jpbm/article/30/5/768/235218/Does-outward-appearance-appeal-to-the-inward-mind). A search summary also reported that glossy food packaging is read as a signal of fatness and that glossy packaging was rated higher on competence (summary only: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7022501/).

**Fleming (2014).** "Visual perception of materials and their properties." *Vision Research* 94, 62-75. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.visres.2013.11.004. Argues perceived gloss comes from low- and mid-level image measures of how far a surface shows specular reflections, rather than estimated physical parameters. A perception paper; it makes no claim about affect. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/24291494)

**Straight versus curved lines and softness.** Albertazzi et al. (above) found straight lines described as hard, rough and sharp and curved lines as soft and fluffy (read: https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC8699471).

---

#### 6. Direction: up, down, left, right

##### 6.1 Vertical position, valence and power

**Meier and Robinson (2004).** "Why the sunny side is up: association between affect and vertical position." *Psychological Science* 15(4), 243-247. https://doi.org/10.1111/j.0956-7976.2004.00659.x
- Three studies of words on a computer. Positive words were evaluated faster in the up position and negative words faster in the down position; positive evaluations activated higher areas of visual space and negative lower; spatial positions did not activate evaluations (Study 3). (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/15043641; participant numbers not in the abstract)

**Meier, Hauser, Robinson, Friesen and Schjeldahl (2007).** "What's 'up' with God? Vertical space as a representation of the divine." *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology* 93, 699-710. https://doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.93.5.699. Six experiments: God-Devil and up-down are implicitly associated; God-related words were encoded faster in a high position; strangers' images in a high position were rated as more likely to believe in God, independent of power and likability inferences. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/17983295)

**Schubert (2005).** "Your highness: vertical positions as perceptual symbols of power." *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology* 89(1), 1-21. https://doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.89.1.1
- Six studies. Study 1: a shared vertical image schema for power. Studies 2 to 4: judgment of a group's power is influenced by the group's vertical position and by motor responses implying vertical movement. Study 5 ruled out valence as the driver of the vertical effect. Study 6: vertical position affects the power judgment result itself. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/16060739; participant numbers not in the abstract)

**Zanolie et al. (2012).** "Mighty metaphors: behavioral and ERP evidence that power shifts attention on a vertical dimension." *Brain and Cognition* 78, 50-58. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.bandc.2011.10.006. After deciding whether a word was a powerful or powerless person, participants identified a target faster when its position (top or bottom) matched the word's power; a larger N1 for congruent positions. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/22088775)

**Moeller, Robinson and Zabelina (2008).** "Personality dominance and preferential use of the vertical dimension of space." *Psychological Science* 19, 355-361. https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1467-9280.2008.02093.x. Two studies, total N = 96: more dominant individuals were faster to respond to probes along the vertical dimension, controlling for horizontal speed. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/18399888)

**Gottwald, Elsner and Pollatos (2015).** "Good is up: spatial metaphors in action observation." *Frontiers in Psychology* 6, 1605. https://doi.org/10.3389/fpsyg.2015.01605. Neutral objects rated more positively when seen placed up than down; the effect was slightly stronger for "bad is down". (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/26539147)

**Where studies disagree on vertical effects**
- **Lakens (2012).** "Polarity correspondence in metaphor congruency effects: structural overlap predicts categorization times for bipolar concepts presented in vertical space." *Journal of Experimental Psychology: Learning, Memory, and Cognition* 38, 726-736. https://doi.org/10.1037/a0024955. A meta-analysis (Study 1) found polarity accounts explain reaction times in earlier studies better than interference accounts; in Study 2, controlling the polarity advantage of positive words removed both the main effect of word polarity and the interaction between word meaning and vertical position. The author concludes these effects "should not be interpreted as automatic associations between vertical locations and word meaning". (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/21843022)
- **Lakens, Semin and Foroni (2012).** "But for the bad, there would not be good: grounding valence in brightness through shared relational structures." *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General* 141, 584-594. https://doi.org/10.1037/a0026468. Six experiments: black ideographs are consistently judged negative; white ideographs represent positivity only when black's negativity is also active (within-participant design, or against a black background). (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/22201411)
- **Giessner and Schubert** magnitude-of-elevation effect failed in Many Labs 2 (above, 4.1).

##### 6.2 Left and right

**Casasanto (2009).** "Embodiment of abstract concepts: good and bad in right- and left-handers." *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General* 138, 351-367. https://doi.org/10.1037/a0015854. Five experiments: right-handers associated rightward space with positive ideas and leftward with negative; left-handers the reverse; the author argues this is not explained by linguistic experience, since English idioms link good with right but not with left. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/19653795)

**Yamada et al. (2025).** "Where the 'bad' and the 'good' go: a multi-lab direct replication report of Casasanto (2009, Experiment 1)." *Memory & Cognition* 53, 1140-1146. https://doi.org/10.3758/s13421-024-01637-1. 2,222 participants in 12 countries; supports that right-handers associate right with positivity and left with negativity, and the reverse for left-handers. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/39313589.) A search summary of the full text says the original's stronger "good is left" pattern in left-handers was not found in the replication (summary only: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12141115/).

**Maass and Russo (2003).** "Directional bias in the mental representation of spatial events: nature or culture?" *Psychological Science* 14, 296-301. https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-9280.14421. Two cross-cultural studies: Italians tended to place the subject of a sentence at left, Arabs at right (Exp. 1); both groups were faster when the subject was in the position of their writing direction; an additional language-independent facilitation for left-to-right action. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/12807400)

**Christman and Pinger (1997).** "Lateral biases in aesthetic preferences: pictorial dimensions and neural mechanisms." *Laterality* 2, 155-175. https://doi.org/10.1080/713754266. Observers showed robust preferences for left-to-right directionality in pictures, especially with left-biased interest; right-biased interest eliminated it; the weight dimension had little effect. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/15513061)

**Walker et al. (2010).** "Preverbal infants' sensitivity to synaesthetic cross-modality correspondences." *Psychological Science* 21, 21-25. https://doi.org/10.1177/0956797609354734. 3- to 4-month-old infants looked longer at a display when changing pitch matched visuospatial height and visual sharpness. The authors call it the strongest indication to date that such correspondences are unlearned. (read: abstract, https://europepmc.org/article/MED/20424017)

---

#### What I could not find or open

- **Adams and Osgood (1973)** and **Kaya and Epps (2004)** full text: the first page was closed to me; the second I read only as an abstract.
- **Palmer and Schloss (2010)** PNAS full text (the 80% figure is relayed by Taylor and Franklin; the proceedings give 67%). **Berlin and Kay (1969)** was not opened; only secondary pages and Kay and Regier (2003).
- **Hevner (1935)**, **Lundholm (1921)**, **Poffenberger and Barrows (1924)**, **Collier (1996)**: none opened; the first three are described only through the 2016 curvature review and a Cognitive Science Society paper. I found no abstract or text for Collier (1996) at all; the one thing the search returned was a second-hand remark (blue chosen for "sad", yellow for "cheerful") in a paper on emotionally mediated synaesthesia, which I did not open.
- **Aronoff, Woike and Hyman (1992)**, **Cotter et al. (2017)**, **Bertamini et al. (2015/2016)**, **Palumbo and Bertamini (2016)** "The curvature effect: a comparison between preference tasks": not opened (publisher pages returned 403 or the indexes did not carry them).
- **Line weight:** no experimental study of thick versus thin drawn line and perceived strength or fragility other than Albertazzi et al. (2021). No study of stroke weight in logos, icons or drawings with emotion ratings. No study I could open on printed thickness and perceived *fragility*; the rating pair that came closest is heavy/lightweight, strong/weak in Albertazzi et al.
- **Size:** no study varying the printed or drawn size of a symbol or figure and measuring felt dominance or importance. Schubert, Waldzus and Giessner (2009), "Control over the association of power and size," *Social Cognition* 27, 1-19, was sought; the index returned nothing and I did not open the paper. Lakens, Semin and Foroni (2011), "Why your highness needs the people," was named in a search summary but I did not read it.
- **Texture:** no replication found of the rough-puzzle result. No large study of gloss versus matte and *felt* emotion (as opposed to attractiveness), as noted by the search summary I read. Etzi, Spence and Gallace (2014) on tactile preferences was not found in the indexes.
- **Direction:** no multi-lab replication of Meier and Robinson (2004) or Schubert (2005) found, other than the Many Labs 2 failure of the Giessner and Schubert vertical-line study. No data on the direction of up-down valence mappings outside Western and a few Asian samples beyond the 12-country Casasanto replication, whose country list I did not extract.
- **Full-text participant numbers** are missing for many of the abstract-only entries above (Valdez and Mehrabian, Schubert 2005, Meier and Robinson 2004, Larson et al. 2007 and 2009).

## Part Four: The table

The nine grahas by visible quality, collecting what Part One's graha table (1a) and Part Two say. It assigns nothing and concludes nothing; a cell is empty where no source speaks. Part One's marks are the codes of the source key in 1a (BPHS-S, BJ-S and the others), each read unless it says memory. Part Two's marks name the author or text and the place: MaP is the Matsyapurāṇa, VdhP the Viṣṇudharmottarapurāṇa, YājS the Yājñavalkyasmṛti, Agni the Agnipurāṇa, Rao T. A. Gopinatha Rao, Pingree and Kropf as in 2b, Hindu system and Nayrizi as in Biruni §389. Readings that differ are separated by semicolons.
